NOTICE.-TO EMPLOYERS OF LABOUR.

I 01 9,---driißf EXHIBITION. 101 t, INTERNATIONAL AND UNIVERSAL.

Save up yo-;t: -1.33—0ne-y—Kn7a—visit to the Continent in 1913. For 14s. return you can go to Ghent,in Belgium, and see the greatest International Exhibition the world has ever produced. In 27 beautiful palaces, situated in the famous flower gardens of the ancient capital of Flanders, there will be displayed all the most wonderful inventions ana produce of the world's civilisation and progress. Communication from England to Ghent is easy. The splendid turbine steamers running three times daily from Dover to Ostend are the last word in travel luxury. The Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway Co. will run their fine boats daily from Hull to Zeebrugge. The magnificent steamers run in connection with the G.E.R. train from Liverpool-street every evening at 8.20 via Harwich, to Antwerp, are too well known to need denription. The London and Continental Steam- ship Co. will also run a palace steamer every evening from London Bridge to Ostend. Excursion arrange- ments are being pushed forward, and week-end tickets are to be issued to Ghent and back, for which 5,000 passengers weekly are already guaranteed during the whole exhibition. The enterprise of the City of Ghent Authorities has provided several big hotels for foreign visitors. The latest addition to the list is the Palace Hotel, facing St. Peter's Station, and at the door of the Exhibition. It contains 1,000 bed rooms, with baths &c., and all the requirements of the modern Parisian or London hotel. There is also the Post Hotel, and the Cour St. Georges, both establishments of last year's construction' built by the town. Besides these,thermare numerous other hotels and pensions, sufficient to accommodate 30,000 daily. Special inducements will be offered to large employers of labour desirous of sending their staff on a Continental educational outing during summer, 1913, for which arrangements must be made long in advance. All interested may apply to KNOTT, KI TCHENER, and CO. (Ltd,), I. SOUTHANWTON-ROW, LONDON, W.C., for full information dealing with the matter. Ghent is an ancient city, with a glorious history, to which testify its numerous monuments,making it one of the most interesting cities in the world to the tourist. It is also known as the City of Flowers, being the centre of European laerticulture. The quinquennial flower shows held there attract visitors from ail parts of the world. The 1913 Exhibition promises to out- shine all previous efforts, as it will be held in the new Palais d'Horticulture, a building one-third bigger than the Crystal Palace, and will open on April 26th, the opening day of the Exhibition. 26 acres of magnificent restaurants and cafZss are distributed throughout the grounds, where one may hove, from the marble palace, with its 10-guinea dinner to the simple cafe, where visitors may obtain penny sandwiches' &c. COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON BYE-LAWS FOR REQUIRING _

===== 0002601_19120907_art0003.txt | included=true | included =====
REAR LIGHTS ON

----- NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the Town Council of the County Borough of Warrington at a Meeting held on Tuesday, the 3rd day of September, 1912, made, under Section 23 of the Municipal Corpora- tions Act, 1882, and Section 5 (3) of the Lights on Vehicles Act, 1907, the following BYE-LAWS, viz. : REAR LIGHTS ON VEHICLES. 1. (1) Every person who shall cause or permit any vehicle to be in any street, highway, or road, to which the public have access, during the period between one hour after sunset and :one hour before sunrise, besides complying with the requirements of the Lights on Vehicles Act, 1907, shall provide such vehicle either (a) with a lamp (or lamps) in proper working order, and so constructed and capable of being so attached as when lighted to show to the rear a red light visible for a reasonable distance; or (b) with a reflector(or reflectors) at least l inches wide, so constructed and capable of being so attached to such vehicle as to reflect a red light from a light carried by a vehicle approaching from behind. If only one lamp or reflector is provided, it shall be placed on the off or right side of the vehicle, or if such vehicle is used for the purpose of drawing behind it any other vehicle or load, on the off or right side of such other vehicle or load. (2) Every person driving or being in charge of such vehicle in any street, highway, or road, to which the public have access during the period as aforesaid, shall keep such lamp or lamps properly attached and properly trimmed and lighted or such reflector or reflectors properly attached and in such condition as to reflect a red light. DEFINITION. . 2. The expression " vehicle "in these Bye-laws shall mean every vehicle, machine or implement to which the Lights on Vehicles Act, 1907, applies. EXTENT AN D_COMMENCEMENT OF

BYE-LAWS. 3. These Bye-laws shall have effect and be in force in the County Borough of Warrington, and shall come into force on the Ist day of January, 1913. PENALTY.

_ 4. If any person offends against these Bye-laws he shall be liable to a penalty not exceeding Forty Shillings, and in the case of a second or subsequent conviction to a penalty not exceeding Five Pounds. Provided that if a person driving or being in charge of a vehicle is charged with an offence against these Bye- laws, he shall not be convicted thereof if he proves to the satisfaction of the Court that such offence arose through the neglect or default of some other person whose duty it was to provide the vehicle with a lamp or lamps or reflector or reflectors. AND NOTICE IS HEREBY FURTHER GIVEN that a sealed copy of such Bye-laws has been forwarded to the Secretary of State. Dated this 7th day of September, 1912. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town clerk,

NORTHERN UNION LEAGUE MEETING.

GILBERT'S REGISTRATION. A meeting of the committee of the Northern Rugby League was held at Huddersfield on Tuesday, Mr. J. B. Cooke (Wakefield Trinity) presidicg. The date of the Salford Reserves v Rochdale Hornets Reserves match was altered from September 7th to March Bth. The following new League fixtures were arranged Salford v. Keighley, March 21st, and Keighley v Salford, March 24th. J. Barton, of Hunslet, who was suspended sine die two years ago for playing with two different clubs under two different names, appeared before the com- mittee and appealed for reinstatement. After hearing the evidence it was decided to reinstate him forthwith. The most important matter before the committee was the fate of the Hull Club and H A Gilbert, the international three-quarter beck. What the exact nature of the objection to Gilberts registration may be did not come out, as the meeting of the committee as usual was held in private. The representatives of the Hull club and Gilbert himself were at the George Hotel in spite of the following teleg,am, sent to Mr Char lesworth, the Hull secretary, by Mr John Clifford, of Huddersfield, a member of the committee, on Monday : " Shall not oppose Gilbert's registration further. Do not attend Tuesday's meeting. Am wiring Houghton." The Hull representative attended prepared to meet any charge, but it was ascertained that Mr Clifford did not desire to proceed with calling the Hull representa- tives into the room. It was decided to defer further consideration of the matter until the next meeting, Gilbert in the meantime being granted permission to play.

FOOTBALL SEASON OPENS.

WARRINGTON'S TRIAL OF STRENGTH AT HUDDERSFIELD.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
A Fine Game and

The Warrington—Huddersfield match was the only one on the card for Monday and provided a rare attrac- tion at Huddersfield, where the Honley feast had liber- ated the operatives from toil. There was a fine opening attendance on the Fartown ground, 10,950 people paying £223 for admission. Both sides relied largely on the teams which played last season, though there was one new man in the home ranks in the person of Howell Davies, the Welsh international forward. War- rington made a notable change at the last minute, for Bryan Rushton, the Halifax half-back, had been signed on during the week-end, and took the place vacated by Daintith at the base of the scrum. In other respects the Warrington team was as selected, , and much speculation was rife among their supporters as to how Jolley, and the light pack in front, would fare against the Huddersfield .talwarts. The weather was windy and showery, and it may be said now that Warrington had the 1% orst of the elements in both halves of the game. The teams lined up as follows : Warringtoe—B Jolley, back ; P Clare, G Dickenson, A Renwick and B Bradshaw, three-quarter backs; E Brooks and Rushton, half backs ; J W Chesters, Tranter, J Arnold, J Fearnley, 11 Skeltiorn and J Critchley, forwards. Huddersfield—M Hatted, hack ; A A Rosenfeld, E Wrigley, H Wagstaffe and W F Kitchen, three-quarter backs ; T H Grey and S Wildman, half backs ; D Clark, Howell J Davies, B Gronow, 3 Higson, F Long- Mafia and W Trevarthen, forwards. Referee Mr A Hesttord, Broughton. Touch judges: Messrs D Fitzgerald, Batley ; and J Bowes, Halifax.

Some Bad Luck.

Colonial in vigorous fashion. Warrington were bard pressed, and a move by Gray and Rosenfeld resulted in a cross kick which beat Jolley by. inches and Grey scored the opening point in good position. The Warrington centres led a raid into Fartown quarters but the borne becks were getting the hall and soon pierced the Werrington defence again. Rosenfeld seas the scorer, and the way he eluded Clare gave Jolley rather too much to manage at the corner. but tbey didn't do it. lt will be a few weeks before Huddersfield aie at their best.

Today, Warrington are due at Leigh and there are high hopes that they will earn their first point. No doubt Leigh are equally hopeful, and possibly more confident. Personally, I think Warrington are a bit heavily handic pped with two opening matches away, and if they beat Leigh it vtill be a feather in their caps. They will be triers to a roan. Practically the same side will do duty, us the injury to Bradshaw merely brings in Waywell who has been paying strict attention to training. The team therefore will be: Jolley, full back ; Wayvvell, Renwick, Dickenson and Clare, three-quarters ; Rushton and Brooks, half backs; Chet.ters, Tranter, Fearnley, Skelhorne,Arnold and Critchley, forwards.

A lot of interest is sure to be taken in to day's "A" team to oppose Leigh "A " at Wilderspool. All but one of the backs are new comers, and half the forwards. Some of these lads are expected to turn out well, and there are others who must have a trial at the first opportunity. Brown, the full back, is standing down through injury, and the ever-ready skipper, Jordan, fills the vacancy. The following is the team : • Jordan, full back ; Warren, Dixon (Seaton), Moore (Batley), and Twist (Widnes), three- quartm s ; Gal'op (Rylands) and Berry (Wigan), half- backs; Bramwood (Bylands), Valentine (Wigan), Ciuise (St. Helens), Cooper, Clark and Gibbs, forwards.

NOTES BY " MUST."

Warrington are at the bottom of the League. Therefore they have every inducement to climb, and after the display at Huddersfield on Monday I feel convinced that their progress will he upward. It was not to be imagined that they would win the opening engagement with the champions, but they gave them a worse shaking than anyone anticipated, and hut for unforseen circumstances there would have been a closer struggle for victory. Huddersfield won by 18 points to three, but they were not value hr such a margin. Last season Warrington were beaten 21-0, so that there is already some improvement to chronicle.

It seems early, and perhaps injudicious, to com- plain of ill•luck. But facts are facts, and there is no doubt that Warrington had all the misfortune there was knocking about Fartown on Monday. The team was the lightest ever, to begin with, but 'alien the unfortunate Bradshaw retired hurt after five minutes play, the blow was enough to destroy one's faith in Providence. The Wirepullers were conceding at least a stone and a half per man in the pack before this mishap, and then they had to take 'Planter out. Common or garden adjectives were innocuous. Losing the toss was a minor matter, but it set Warrington against wind and sun. On changing ends the weather did a change also, and the unlucky visitors had wind and rain in their faces for the Con- cluding 40 minutes. They must surely have been getting the worst over in one instalment !

The score is no criterion of Warrington's display even in such adverse conditions. They shared like a team that will "come on " rapidly, and if the full thirteen had lasted the game out, Hudderbfield might have been hard pressed to win. The Warrington lads were in better condition, and the way the five young forwards held their own with, and even overplayed, the big sextette opposite was an eye-opener. They were heroes, every one of there, and it is no secret that they surprised themselves as well as 111,-ir supporters. Of course, Huddersfield were the cleverer team behind, but sufficient was seen of the Warring- tort backs to indicate that they should be more forceful on the attack than last year. There were weak spots, but there is promising material at hand.

Great interest was taken in the appearance of Rushton, the new half back from Halifax, who has already worn the colours of Batley and Barrow in addition. Such a record of migration is not ebcourag- ing but it does not say that Warrington have not made a useful capture. Rushton shaped well con- sidering the strange company and his want of pre- Duration. Ile was nippy and got the ball away smartly. Brooks has had worse partnere, and the new comer may yet do well. Of the international it , can be said that he showed fine form, and seems in better trim than he has been for a year or two. At half back Warrington are going to be stronger. Nothing could be more gratifying.

Then there was Jolley. The young full back was severely tired in such classy company, but he came rut of the ordeal with a reputation which will grow. After a shaky start he played like an old hand, and in the second half he was brillisat. There is real football in this youngster, which is only to be expected from a Runcorn Jolley. He will improve every much or lam greatly mistaken. He has ideas of attack as well as defence, which is what we we have long lacked in our full backs.

The Huddersfield men seemed short of training, and several were carrying a lot of weight. Wrigley was huge, and it was a joke at times to see the diminutive Jolley bring him down by the legs. On another occasion Jolley and Wagstaffe came down together and burst the ball. The youngster has a grip, as more than one of the big Fartown backs can testify. Forward they were big enough to eat Warrington,

NORTON.

The canal here had rather-More than the ordinary number of anglers and we noticed that many were novices out for the first time, purchasing their privilege from Mr Williams at Norton Bridge. It was a Pity the canal was not on, as there is nothing more pleasing for a first timer than to catch a few and it encourages them to join a Society, but when they find fish off they seem to th:nk the game is uot won h the candle. WESLEYAN CYCLING CLUB.

We were pleased on Saturday to find that Lord Tatton of Egerton has removed the ban on caterers for cyclists and others at Castle Mill. Twenty-three started from Latchford Swing Bridge with a following wind through Lymm, Bucklow. Hill, Ilostherne and over Ashley Station. At Castle Mill, the favourite holiday centre of Altrincham, the only place where parties could obtain teas was, until very lately, the old mill house, and they, I believe, did it in defiance of his Lordship and at terrible risk. Now, however, Iseveral houses on either side the river are open to 6receive visitors, and the party stayed at Chapel House Farm, where Mrs. Overenii made us very comfortable. The return was made through the splendid roads of the Brooklands estate to Brooklands Station and home through Altrincham —35 miles. Lamp time to-night, 7.45 ; next Saturday, 7.29.

THE TRADE UNION CONGRESS.

This week the Trade Union Congress has been meeting at Newport (Mon.). There are 500 delegates attending, representing nearly 2,000,000 organised workers. The Congress is presided over by Mr. Will Thorne, M.P. A number of new and important issues hive been raised for the first tithe. One of these

is the question of Syndicalism. Another is the proposal of the London compositors that the Congress should organise a movement to counteract the increasing cost of living, by securing an all-rosnd increase of ss. per week in the .wages of the working classes.

THE CYCLIST'S NOTE-BOOK.

[By " Sliver Street."' LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE.

I did not expect to have such an early and such a noteworthy confirmation of my contention that Cheshire still extends over the Canal to the Mersey as appeared in all the Warrington papers last Saturday. In giving notice of the dates when the Parliamentaty lists will come before his court, the Revising Barrister for this district calls upon all persons concerned to attend on such a date at the Hall Cafe Stockton Heath to claim votes for "Stockton Heath, Walton Inferior, Walton Superior, and Warrington, that part within the Geographical County of Chester." So that some high officials need their geography revising as well as yours truly, if the opening sentence in the " Notes "I am referring to is correct, viz., "It is a fairly regular duty of the staff of this office to explain that Warrington is 'all Lancashire, ' and that no part of the borough lies in Cheshire."

===== 0002601_19120907_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
A PENNY SPANNER.

I had shown me a week or two ago, a useful tool made of stout steel wire, the ends bent so as to serve as a spanner for locking cone 3 that work with holes in the side. I have seen them before but this newer one is much stronger, and cf better finish. It is worth a penny, anyway. A CLEVER REPAIRER.

Talking of wire reminds me of -a----da:Y when I took my machine for a small repair, and found the agent trying to straighten some wire with his fingers. After watching him for five minutes I asked him if he ever tried with his foot. " With my foot ? How ? " So I drew it between my heel and block of wood. "Well, I never " and he laughed. Needless to bay that wasn't in Warrington.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXE VALLEY.

The road fro-m—Ex-e-ter—tO 'iline—h-ea.d runs indirect north, as one might say. You could rule a straight line between the two towns lying due north and south, from side to side of Devonshire, and it would cross the river a dozen times. The beauty of the valley for the cyclist is that it would cross the road too. For forty miles we cycled up the river, rising 20 to 080 feet, and had no need to dismount once, and yet we jumped off scores of times for the flowers and the pictures. At Tiverton (15 miles) we were surprised to find a huge factory, and the nose from inside was like unto Cockbedge. But every part was so clean and new-looking that I was constrained to ask a boy what they produced there. He horrified me by saying " Loice. I said " surely not " ; but it was even so. Honiton is only a few miles away, and Tiverton joins it in making lace, not on pillows alone, but by the thousand yards. From Tiverton to Exton where the roads begins to climb over Exmoor the valley is a dream. The road follows every curve of the stream, for there is no other way. The woods crowd down to the water's edge all the way, deep slumberous woods that would use up your plates by the score, if you did not keep a tight band on your camera bag. We missed Hampton and then Dalverton by a mile and noted the spot where the Barba joins the Exe, for we were just on the edge of the Lorna Boone country. We found no less than five different kinds of orchid growing in the grass by the roadside, without penetrating the woods at all, and the beautiful scabious, and agrimony and " Golden rod " ragwort supplied blue and gold in abundance. From Wheddon Cross to Dunster is only six miles and the counterpart of the whole rise of the after- noon was now taken in the splendid coast down the Avill valley. Dunster is very picturesque. Its parish church is like a small cathedral; its old market house, the old nunnery, the colour of its houses and roofs, the tower, the castle, and the sea front, which has no honses,nor promenade nor niggers, nor truffle', but is crowded with many different sea side plants, prominent amongst which are the fine yellow flowers of the great horned poppy, and the road to which is adorned with scores of walnut trees, all these make the little Somerset town worthy of the name and fame of two Ilfracomhes.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
LOAFERS AND LIBRARIES.

Warrington Librarian and Businessmen's Grievance.

'!At the conference of the Library Association, held in Liverpool on Thursday, Mr. Stallwood, of Reading referred to the misuse of reading-rooms. Be said he remembered reading in several London papers remarks upon reports which had been submitted by librarians to their committees. One report stated that the greatest offenders in the reading-rooms were ladies, who used them as dressing-rooms, and, in wet weather, even to change their garments. (Laughter.) These reports were freely commented upon, and he ventured to sug- gest to librarians that it would be wiser if matters of 1 this kind were not introduced into the reports, or, if they were, that the Town Clerks should see that they were kept ont of the minutes. (ApplauEe.) By using their powers librarians could prevent the misuse of reading-rooms without making the matter public. Mr Fraser, of Aberdeen, did not consider librarians were troubling their heads about adverse criticism. Tne proper way to deal with criticism was, if possible, to do their work better. It was their duty as librarians to get at the young people who were just leaving school, when their imagination was looking round for fresh fields of study. They wanted not merely to train the children in right thinking, but to plant within their minds right ideals. In Aberdeen they made a special study of the child-en's side of library work. A hall had been taken and equipped as a reading-room, their only difficulty being experienced in stocking it with periodicals. In his opinion, the periodicals for children in England 'ere wholly inadequate. They in Aberdeen had been obliged to get their stock from America. Mr Madeley, of Warrington, contended that librarians s'^ould do all they could to eliminate the loafer from the library. Many times business risen refrained from entering a library because the loafer was idling over the papers. Mr Charles Jones, of Kensington, opposed the idea of cutting down the works of fiction in libraries. They represented the flights of imagination of the greatest men of all time, and if some of the fiction to-day was not so good, then it was their duty to see that the com- parisons stimulated young men and women to the accomplishment of better things.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KIMOC CHIEF "RUNNING ELK."

On the Use of Stone Hammers.

One of the finest specimens of aboriginal humanity is the Chief Running Elk. As white men count time, he is 44 years old—as he states the case, the grass has grown four ten times and four single times additional times since he first batted an inquiring eye at the sun. The native numeration does not proceed beyond ten. Twenty is two tens—forty is four tens. There is a division as to how to proceed beyond the century point, but for many years the discussion on this point has been in the nature of an argument in the abstract. No Ki moo has grown so plutocratic as to own as many as 100 ponies, and there has been no occasion, therefore, for a study of higher mathematics. Running Elk appeared in an amazing red shirt, blue leggins, and a heavy blue blanket. Ile swung negli- gently in his left hand a stone hammer or maul, a weapon which distinctly lethal in its uses, is not with- out crest value for decorative purposes. It is shaped as if two cones of equal size were placed together. A rawhide thong is twisted about the middle, and, con- tinued forms the handle, about two feet in length. This is covered with beds, which serve a double purpose— it appeals to the Kimioc love for beauty and likewise sufficiently strengthens and stiffens the handle to make the weapon:one teat is well to dodge. How does it com- pare with a hand axe as an instrument of slaughter? was asked of Running Elk. " It is a good thing " he replied. Did you ever make a practical test of its efficacy? If so, how many men have you killed with it? Running Elk smiled dreamily, as one who was living in memory of joys of long ago. At last he replied : "I cannot recall on the spur of the moment any men that I have killed with this instrument. It is a sure thing with a sheep or with a steer, when one uses it discriminatingly. I remember when I was a boy that the old men of our tribe would tell stories of the use of the stone hammer in battle, and how they put their enemies into a per- manent sleep by its means. As I said, I cannot now call to mind that I have killed anyone with this. In my judgment, however, it has many points of superior- ity over the steel tomahawk, or carpenter's hand axe. This hammer would not spoil the hair, for instance, and will not glance from the skull. One fair stroke and it is all oil, whereas your hatchet is an uncertain thing. A slight obstruction may deflect it." As he talked Running Elk illustrated his discourse by indicating a point on the questioner's head, just back of the ear, where he said a judicious belt with the stone hammer would produce good results in ninety- nine instances out of a possible hundred. " Taking everything into consideration," he concluded," for good reliable work give me the stone hammer." Wirh these remarks he left the writer and fixed himself comfortably among the Indian curiosities.

HOUSING THE WORKERS. AN ESTATE TO BE DEVELOPED AT ALTRINCHAAL

The Altrincham Council has decided to cover a large field, about four acres in extent, which was purchased a few years ago, with 56 houses for working men of small means, under the Houses of the Working Classes Act. The development of the estate has commenced, and the general design of the works, %Mr h has been prepared by Mr II E Brown, the surveyor, has been approved by the Council. The cost of the scheme is expected to amount to upwards of £ll,OOO, and the loan for the purpose has been arranged. The rents fixed by the Council are expected to cover interest and the repayment of the principal, as well as the Inhofe of the working expenses.

COMMERCIAL ' FAlLCRES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the number of commercial failures published in Great Britain and Ireland during the five weeks ending Saturday, August 31st, was 675. The number in the corresponding five weeks of last year was 677, showing a decrease of 2, being a net decrease in 1912, of 275.

AYWARD GIRL. A .ory of bow a young daughter involved members of her own family as well as her soldier lover in Police Court pe.ceedings of a serious nature was unfolded at the Warrington Police Court on Monday morning when, before the Mayor (in the chair) and other magistrates two sisters named Edith Hodson and Alice Hodson, domestic servants, of 51, Fox-street, were charged with having stolen, on Sunday, August 18th, or Monday, August 19th, £25 in cash, the money of James Oxley and others. Joseph Matthew Bell, a private in the 2nd Battallioo of the Northumberland Fusiliers stationed at Hillborough Barracks, Sheffield, was charged with receiving £ll in cash from Edith Hodson on August 23rd, well knowing the money to have been stolen. Mary Jane Hodson, 51, Fox-street, the mother of Edith Hodson and Alice Hodson, was charged with receiving £3, and Thomas Hodson, their brother, who is a private iu the York and Lancaster Regiment, was charged with receiving £l. All the prisoners pleaded guilty. The case as fully outlined by Chief Inspector Williamson was a very sad one. From his statement ' it appeared that on the 15th August Edith Hodson was engaged by the Oxleys as housekeeper. She then took up her duties, and at that time there was £25 in gold in various drawers in the bedrooms, She -sag lett in the house by herself for two hours on the following Monday. On the return of a nurse girl it was found that the girl Edith had disappeared. The money was mit.sed and information having been given to the police, it was found that the prisoner Alice had called at the house, and both girls had left Warrington by train. Subsequently the Sheffield police were informed of the affair, and it was stiegestea that probably tbe girl would get in touoh with Bell. Such was the case, and on August 23rd both girls were arrested by the Sheffield police. Edith wae only found to have a purse contain- ing 123 3d and the purse which had contained the stolen money. She at first refused to give any infor- mation at all about the matter. Detective-Sergeant Holland proceeded to Sheffield rind saw the two girls. Edith at first said she did not know what had become of the remainder of the money, and Alice s iid she did not know what her sister had done with it. She knew it had been stolen from Oxley's. On the Saturday morning Alice informed the police that Bell had the remainder of the momey. She saw her sister giving him a lot of gold the previous night, and he promised to hand it over to the police. Bell then stoutly denied having had any of the money and he was taken to the Sheffield police station where be was found in pomession of a new silver wrist watch, which, be said he had purchased with a sovereign of his own money. Having been brought back to Warrington along with the other two prisoners, Edith said she took the money and gave it to Bell who she understood was going to give it to the police. Bell still denied having received any of the money but a tearful appeal on the part of Edith resulted in his say- ing" Well, yes, lass,[ have the money but it is only £9 ; it is at Shrine-1(i et the barracks now. A comrade of mine has it and he will not give it up until I telegraph him." The money was subsequently found in a pair of gloves. He (the inspector) hod gone with Detective Sergeant Holland to see the mother of the girls, about the stolen money. After repeated denials the mother was confronted by her daughters at the police station. Edith said (A` Now mother you might as well give it up. This police know all about it. I gave £3 to you in the kitchen." The mother then admitted having had the money and said she had only £1 left. She had warned Edith and at first did not want the money. The mother baying dis- closed the hiding place where the sovereign was, he (the inspector) returned to the house and found the money behind a board in the coal abed. In reply to the charge the mother said "I did not know it was stolen." The brother denied receiving the sovereign at first but afterwards said Edith bad given him £1 and had told him she had £2O from the house, He was found to have 9s led in his possession whereas he had said he only had Is. on him. The two girls Edith and Alice and the brother Thomas had nothing further to say than that they were guilty. Mrs Hodson raid she did not know the money had been stolen. Bell told a long story in which he said he did not know at first that the money was stolen. Had it not been for hirn the girls would not have been found DOC any of the money recovered. He had found the girls character worse than he had thought it to be. The mother of the two girls tearfully appealed for leniency. Chief Inspector Williamson said the family were about to move front Warrington to Sheffield. The mother had previously been a highly-respectable woman. The two girls had never been charged with anything ex,ept that Edith had been Jocked up in Liverpool for drunkenness, but she was discharged. From the age of fourteen to fifteen they appeared to have got out of the control of their parents. Edith had also been in a home. The girls had been going from situation CO situ-lion. They did not appear to realize their position, for they had treated the case lightly in the cells. Private Bell had an excellent character before be joined the service, and Private Hodson bad two years' service in the Army and was under orders for abroad. He had a clean record. The whole of the prisoners owed their position to the prisoner Edith Hodson. The Bench retired to consider their decision, and on their return the Chairman said the magistrates were of opinion that Edith Hodson was the prime mover in that most heartless theft. It was most heartless to take the life savings of those young men. She would have to go to prison for three months with bard labour. The other prisoners were bouud over for twelve months to come up for judgment when called upon.

PUDDLER PUNISHED. TWO MONTHS FOR MAN AND WIFE.

At the City Police Court, Manchester, on Thursday, Martin Mylett, who said he was a puddler, of Warring- ton, and Janet Mylett, his wife, were charged with stealing a gold watch and chain, valued at £ll from Cranford Lodge, Higher Crumpsall, Manchester. Detective-Sergeant Warwick said prisoners were arrested by Detective-Sergeant Reddington of the Warrington police. Both denied the charge and said the chain (produced) was picked up by the female prisoner between Bedford Leigh and Lowton St. Mary's. Mrs Redfern, wife of prosecutor, said the articles were missed after prisoners had visited the house. The chain was undoubtedly the property of witness' who identified it by it having been broken and repaired in two places. Joseph Henry Houghton, pawnbroker, Mersey-street, Warrington, identified prisoners as the persons who visited his shop on August 21 and pawned the chain for 30s. Witness believed the chain was the property of the prisoners, and did nit hesitate to lend the amount named.

Each prisoner was sent to gaol for two months with hard labowr.

Borough Police Court. TCESDAY.—Before Councillor Roberts (in the chair), Alderman James Evans, Councillor C Jackson Holmes, Messrs J Rymer Young and J Hepberd. BRUTAL ASSAULT.—Nathan Sankey, 1, Hopwood- street was charged with having assaulted May Hornung of School Brow, on August 24th. The Chief Constable stated that at about 9 p.m. on the day in question the prisoner went to the house of the prosecutrix. The complainant said the prisoner assaulted her with fire- irons and seizing a knife, said he would cut her throat. He put his hand over her mouth to prevent her scream- ing. Detective-Sergeant Holland stated that he saw the prisoner ab ut the assault. Sankey said he believed Mrs Hornung asked him in the house. He remembered going in the house, but recollected nothing after. In answer to the charge be said " I don't think I did assault her." Prisoner denied being at the house of the prosecutrix on the date in question and told many tales of drinking escapades in the complainant's house. The Chief Constable said be thought the drink was respon- sible. Sankey had also treated his mother brutally. Sankey vas sent to gaol for six months. NEGLECT OF CHILDREN—John Griffin, 32, .Tames- Street, was charged with having neglected his six children in a manner likely to cause them suffering or injury to health.-141r. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the Society and said the prisoner had been under the supervision of Inspector Wainwright for two years, and received constznt warnings as to his conduct. When in drink he would turn his wife and children into the street. On one occasion the wife went to complain to a constable. The constable went pa the house and found the door locked. The prisoner, who was drunk, was asleep inside. The officer put One of the boys through tbe window in order that be might open the door. About 9.30 on Sunday night last the prisoner put his food under the table and on the wife remon- strating with him he picked up a hatchet and said "I will do for the lot of you." The wife and children ran out of the house. The children were so terrified at the conduct of the man that something would have to he done with him—The wife and Inspector Wainwright corroborated.--The case was adjourned for a month. before they could he settled they would have to build a house in the middle of a field. From the prisoner's statements, it appeared that Mrs Sutcliffe had been living with them and the bother was caused through the male prisoner trying to make it peaceful for lint Sutcliffe.—Each prisoner was fined 10s. and costs or in default 14 days imprisonment. PRISON PETS.

[COPYRIGHT.]

It is one of the universal laws of nature that to man solitude is unnatural. Even in cases where a religious exaltation has caused men to retire into desert places and there dwell in apparent isolation for years, the isolation has been no more than apparent; for the hermits of old walked continually in a wonderful and mystic world of visions, communing with saints and martyrs day by day. Even those who, like Tinton of Athens, forswore the company of their fellow-men in a mood of savage cynicism, were generally the victims of a diseased mind, or temporarily possessed by a mood of abnormal egotism. Such is the case of those who volun- tarily seek solitude; but what of those who are compelled to spend lonely and monotonous hours within the four walls of a prison cell ? History has many curious records of the strange com- radeships which have sprung up under such conditions—the fruit of the natural yearning of humanity both for diversion and for com- panionship. DUMB COMPANIONS.

Many and many a prisoner, dragging out squalid and friendless years in the Fleet or the Alarshalsea, found a strange solace in the presence of a caged bird, feeling perhaps a cer- tain sympathy for this feathered occupant of a prison within a prison. And further back in the ,dark record"... of history there are numerous instances of those who, rotting through hopeless years in the utter solitude of mediwval dun- geons, have striven at once to provide some living companion, however humble, and to find distraction for the brain tormented by unoccu- pied loneliness to the verge of madness, by the taming oF. creatures naturally distasteful to man, and, averse from his company. Pats, beetles, toads, and spiders are among the creatures which have from time with infinite care and patience been brought under the in- fluence of imprisoned men. For such State Prisons as was the Tower of London in olden times, there was, of course, with regard to certain captives a far greater latitude allowed in the keeping of pet animals than could be accorded in the case of ordinary criminals, either then or now. Noble prisoners confined on mere suspicion, or by reason of the likelihood of their forming a nucleus of disaffec- tion, often, as we know, lived in comparative comfort in prison, even as a first-class mis- demeanant does in our own time. The unhappy King Henry VI., when incarcerated in the Tower, was allowed the company of a favourite cat and dog; aml it is likely enough that they showed a far greater degree of fidelity to the gentle monarch thlin his human subjects and friends.

The unhappy- Lady Hertford, younger sister of Lady Jane Grey, whose secret marriage with Lord Hertford, contrary to Queen Elizabeth's wishes, caused her to spend six weary years in captivity until she died in her prison, ws allowed to keep pets about hcr. The Lieutenant of the Tower, writing to Cecil on the subject of the furniture supplied to Lady Hertford in her apartments at the Tower, describes it as "old, worn, broken and decayed," adding, how- ever, the corollary that Lady Hertford's monkeys and dogs had helped to reduce it to that condition. CATS AS CATERERS.

It was, however, the victims of precautionary detention, as a rule, rather than those in rigorous confinement, who were 'allowed this extent of liberty with regard to their four- footed dependents. There are one or two touch- ing stories told of animal friends of prisoners in the Tower who were kept under a more rigid surveillance. During the reign of King Mallard 111., Sir Henry Wyatt was imprisoned in the fortress, "once," to quote from the Wyatt papers, " in a cold and narrow tower, where he had neither bed to lie on, nor meat for his mouth. He had starved then, had not God, who sent a crow to feed his prophet, sent this and his. country's martyr a cat both to feed and warm him. It was his own relation unto them from whom I had it. A cat came one day down into the dungeon unto him, and; as it were, offered herself unto him. He was glad of her, laid her upon his bosom to warm him, and, by making much of her, won her love. After this she would come every day unto him (livers times, and when she could get one, bring Nim a pigeon. He complained to his keeper of his cold and short fare. The answer was 'he durst not better it,' 'But,' 6aid. sir Henry if I can provide any, will you promise .to dress it for me?' 'I may well enough,' said the keeper; 'you are safe for that matter:' and, being urged again, promised him and kept his promise; dressed for him, from time to time, such pigeons as his caterer the cat pro- vided for him. Sir Henry Wyatt, in his pros- perity, for this would ever make much of cats, as other men will of their spaniels or their hounds; and perhaps you shall not find his picture anywhere, but like Sir Christopher Hatton with his dog, with a cat beside

• Sir Henry had a pOrtrait painted afterwards of the cat, holding a pigeon in its claws, and offering it to him through the grating of his prison window. There is a somewhat similar story told of the Earl of Southampton, who was imprisoned in the Tower for two years for his complicity in the Earl of Essex's conspiracy against Queen Elizabeth. In his case it was a favourite cat

OT .ms own which found it way to him in km solitude, descending into his room by way of the chimney. The faithful beast remained with its master as his companion in adversity, and on his release in 1603 Southampton had hie, portrait painted with his cat beside him: and there is a portrait of him at Welbeek Abbey which may be the one in question.

TAME RODENTS.

In the pricons of the old days there were, however, far worse depths of misery; and it was in the damp of noisome dungeons, deep under the ground, slimy, dark, and fetid, veritable liv- ing tombs, that men used to be driven to tame the horrible sharers of their habitations. Prac- tically all buildings used as prisons used to be overrun with rats. 'The thickness of the IN.-ails, their situation, often either surrounded by a moat or so as to be defended by the water of a river or of the sea, and last, but not least, their defective sanitation, Tendered such buildings as the hostile, the Castle of Chinon, or the Cita- del prisons of Rome, the likeliest of breeding grounds for vermin. It was only natural that the holiness of the creatures should often at- tract the prisoner to attempt to tame them. Rienzi, when imprisoned by the Pone of Avig- non. spent much of his time in feeding the rats which swarmed in the tower where he was I confined; he had names for each, and come to recognise at a glance their separate little in- dividualities. • TIIE INFLUENCE, OF PETE.

A strange phenomenon which has been noted by several observers of the criminal mind is the regard sometimes shown by the most brutal of criminals towards queer animal friends. A curious instance of this strange contradiction in human nature is shown in the story of one Alejandro Guevarra, a Spaniard, who committed a remarkably ghastly murder on Hamp- stead Heath. This man, when in prison awaiting trial, tamed a rat, which he carried about his skin, fastened • to his person with a cord made of his own hair. The creature was on the most friendly terms with him, showing no sign of fear; and Guevarra begged to be allowed to keep it, saying "he loved it so- dearly." When, at last, time rat, which he called " Pobrecita," came to an untimely end at. the paws of one of the prison cats. Guevarra in a transport of fury seized the cat by the throat and strangled it with his own hands. This same man also spent a great deal of time feeding the Fparrows at his cell-window, and they showed a remarkable willingness to respond to his ad- vances. Yet this man was a particularly brutal ! and callous criminal.

In some of the French prisons queer pats used to be allowed. A French convier, whose crime was complicity in the murder of his two brothers once possessed a taMe field-mousia. which he had taught to turn a tiny grindstone. When it died the man was so much affecteil that for some days his life was de4paired of. A convict at Mewl bad a parrot whirl) lio reared: and which seemed to sympathise with his every mood. When he showed sadness, the bird would put its head on onia.side with a ridi- culous affectation of sorrow; while if hs laughed it was always ready to go into ecstacies of mirth. There are, of course, examples of animals hav- ing been turned to account by prisoners as go- betweens. One such amusing case was that of a warder's dog in an Italian prison. This dog, who had long curly hair. was made much , of by some of the prisoners; and one day it was discovered that his curly coat was being made ; the Teeeptable for all manner of ol)ieets for the inmates of the prison. In his daily walks, the dog Wll6 patted by the friends of prisoners and letters. money, tobacco, even knives. had been successfully smuggled into the gaol through this innoemt and unsusnected agent!

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY SEPTEMBER 7, 1912.

BOATS FOR ALL.

The Board of Trade has lost no time in act- ing upon the terrible lessons of the great dis- aster. It is true, of course, that the Depart- ment is but doing those things which ought to have been done many years ago, but few will wish at this stage to dwell unduly on past sins of omission. It is never too early to mend; in many senses, alas! it is now too late ; but the least which the authorities could do was to make provision which shall compass all that human foresight and human skill can accomplish to reduce to the finest minimum the dangers of the sea to life and limb. The new regulations now issued in draft form by the President of the Board of Trade are mainly on the lines which we have anticipated. In the case of ocean-going ships sufficient lifeboats must be carried to accom- modate all on board, while in the case of coasting vessels and cross-Channel ships life-saving appliances must be provided for every person. These provisions are, of course, the only foundation possible for the new order of things. Anything less would have been as inadequate in fact, as it would have been gravely unsatisfactory to the public conscience.lt is important to observe that the existing General Rule 12 which provides that a lesser number of boats might be provided in the event of the ship being divided into efficient watertight compartments, is abro- gated. It is gratifying to fiud it thus expressed that the powers that be—for the present,at any rate—are not prepared to give ear to those who urge that it would be

better to trust in improved methods to make ships unsinkable than to provide boats for all to meet the last emergency. The regula- tions, of course, include a host of other important provisions, but the public will be most relieved to know that the great prin- ciple of boats for all has been firmly adopted in the case of ocean-going steamers. In regard to wireless telegraphy and the speed of steamships in ice regions, Mr. BUXTON agrees with Lord MERSEY that a common international line of action should be agreed on. A bill for the compulsory institution of "wireless " on certain classes of ships has been prepared, but he prefers not to proceed with the measure until after the proposed international negotiations, provided that that course will not entail undue delay.

After drawing attention "SOUND for days "to the serious ENTERTRISE." injury caused to the British motor industry by the great advance of cheap American cars," the "Daily Express" on August 28th printed what is described as "an excellently devised advertisement of an excellent American car." The leading editorial article of the same issue displayed some anxiety about the morality of thus booming a thing in the paid-for columns and wishing it else- where in the new columns. Here is an uneasy passage from the Editor's own pen: " Why do you print these advertisements," cries the critic, "when at the same time you are lamenting the progress of the American car in this country ? " " Why not? Our advertisement columns are open to any sound commercial enter- prise, and we see no reason in the world why the I legitimate enterprise of Messrs Studebaker should be checked by any foolish attempt to debar them from advertising. We should be glad indeed to see them pay a duty on their imported cars, and so in a measure make it easiey. for the British manufacturers to compete with them. But since they are permitted to bring in these cars free of duty, it is at least gratifying to know that they are willing and enterprising enough to spend their money on English advertisements." So the " dumping ' of American goods, over which our Tariff Reformers get so angry, is a "sound and legitimate commercial enter- prise" when the profits from it go to swell the revenue of a Tariff Reform newspaper! Notice also the milky mildness of the refer- ence to the so-called cure for "dumping." The "Express " would be glad to see the Americans pay " a duty "—there is nothing ,to say how big a one—and so "in a measure " make it " easier " for the British manufacturer, and so on; but with nothing to indicate how much easier or in what measure. Of course, the " Express" would not like to see these American cars sternly shut out of our markets so long as they help to keep Tariff Reform newspapers afloat. Even American dollars are good enough to keep, when you've got them.

In a speech made to DOING JUSTICE TO the Cumberland branch TARIFF REFORM, of the Tariff Reform League. Mr. George Wyndham added remission of the Insurance " tax " to the bribes which the Tory party is' prepared to offer to the electors in order to obtain acceptance for a Protectionist tariff. It has strained the ingenuity of Tariff Reform statisticians to the breaking-point to produce any plausible calculation which would pro- duce enough revenue from a tariff upon manufactured articles to make possible the remissions on tea. sugar, &c., which, in Mr. Chamberlain's scheme,were to compensate the workingman for taxes upon his bread-and- butter. Mr. Wyndham, quite undeterred by a difficulty which has for the last year or two made Tariff Reformers noticeably tacti- turn about the character of the " scientific tariff" to be set up by a Tariff Reform Chan- cellor of the Exchequer, has boldly promised, on behalf of his Party, that a tax on manu- factured goods imported into this country will be sufficient—(l) to provide for an extension of Naval expenditure which would satisfy the Jingo notions of what constitutes adequate protection of our shores; (2) to remit taxes on "articles of daily consumption which we cannot produce," in compensation for the increased cost of living caw:6d by a Protedtive tariff; (3) to "relieve the monstrous iniquity of the Insurance Act." And Mr Chamberlain had wired to Mr. Wyndham in anticipation of his speech :—"I am sure that you will do justice to your cause and further the great cause of Tariff Reform."

In this breathing-space A CHAPTER OF in an acute political ABSURDITIES. controversy, there is

an appropriate oppor- tunity to review the ground and ascertain how the case stands between the opposition and the Government. Certainly the situa- tion is a perplexing one. The Opposition demands an immediate appeal to the nation. for the reason that the Government has introduced a Home Rule Bill without having put Home Rule, as a definite issue, before the electorate. But when the opportunity of putting the Home Rule question to a con- stituency arises, through a by-election, the Opposition makes, at most, a mere "feint," and treats Home Rule as a very subsidiary matter. The Opposition, whilst accusing the Government of having set up a single chamber system, boasts that, owing to the delay which the system set up by the Government allows, Home Rule will never pass into law, while the power of delay which the House of Lords might have exercised in regard to the Insurance Bill was never exercised. Yet the Opposition denounces the " monstrous iniquity" of the Insurance Act. The Opposition pledges itself to amend drastically the Insurance Act, but will give no hint of the amendment which it proposes. The Opposition keeps Tariff Reform in as remote a back-ground as possible at by-elections, yet Tariff Reform is the "first plank " in its " constructive pro- gramme."

If the impossibility of AN OBJECT-LESSON. frarning a "scientific tariff" which would produce the revenue required for the normal Y the leaders da, needs of the State, apart even from the wild opfromthisees Tariff ffng Raeorbfoumt bpropagan had not prompted the Tory Party's desire to tell the 'electors as little as possible about the details of Tariff Reform, there is another consideration which must give them pause.; In Germany, under a "scientific tariff," the ; agitation against the artificially enhanced cost of living is assuming enormous propor- tions. Tariff Reformers have been fond of

===== 0002601_19120907_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
appealing to the experience of foreign coun- tries n proof of the blessings which their fiscal "reform " would introduce into this country. Not only in Germany, but in almost every Protectionist country,a striking object-lesson of the dangers which "Tariff Reform" would plunge us into, is ready for all those who accept the invitation to look around and see what a "scientific tariff" , would do for us.

The Chief Burgomaster AGAINST A RISING of Frankfurt, a member TIDE. of the Prussian House

of Peers, has addressed a manifesto to his Government, urging that the time has come for the duties on cattle and meat to be removed or diminished, and the importation of frozen meat to be rendered possible. "Prices of all kinds of slaughter- able beasts have reached an unparalleled height," he declares, and a further rise in prices must be expected. German public opinion is in a state of acute irritation against those fiscal devices of the very kind which the Tariff Reformers ask us from foreign examples to copy in this country. The tide is rising against the evils of a Protectionist system in all parts of the world. It is not exactly a propitious moment, therefore, for the advocacy of Protection in this country. But Tariff Reform is still the "first plank ' in the Tory " constructive programme."

Tariff Reformers en-

WHERE DO THE deavour to evade the FARMER AND FARM- plain, direct lessons to LABOURER COME IN? be drawn from foreign experience, it is not their purpose, they explain, to rise the price of agricultural products in this country Imperial Preference to Canadian wheat and Australasian meat has no ulterior motive of benefiting British agriculturists. This is good news for the weather-worried agricul- turist in this country, of course He is bound to be delighted at the prospect of having to pay higher prices for his imple- ments, clothes, and every article of use or convenience, while not he, but the Canadian wheat-grower and Australasian sheep- farmer get all the benefit. It is no wonder that the Tory Party fights shy of arguing the fiscal question to-day on the platform at by-elections. Food-taxes, which the Tariff Reformer declares will only put money in the pockets of Colonial farmers, are not likely to be popular either with an urban or 1 an agricultural consistency.

LNOTES AND NOTIONS.

"I can quite imagine that, in this weather especially, the sight of a mackintosh was too much for a man of not very high moral standard."—West London magistrate to a man who stole a mackintosh.

- Tradesmen will assuredly find much material for thought in the article on another page entitled "Tradesmen and the Taxation of Land 'Values." There is a remedy here for "hard times" which they may never have contemplated. * *

* * Councillor Wilson said on Tuesday that "he knew gentlemen sitting in the Council Chamber who, if they had to work as an ordinary- man outside, would not get thirty ' bob ' a week." The taunt came with ill- grace from a working-man councillor who enjoys the courtesy position and pecuniary emoluments of Mayor's Auditor. * *

Congratulations to the Rev. John Gunnell on the completion of 50 years in the Wesleyan Ministry and 23 years in the Warrington Circuit. Like the Rev. John Yonge, Mr. Gunnell enjoys the warm affection and esteem of a large circle of friends. These two venerable pastors of a flock innumerable are rightly 'looked upon as the fathers of local Nonconformity,

'* The Women's Tariff* Reform League went to New Brighton on Thursday headed by what the "Liverpool Courier" calls "Lady Greenall's Band" and five flags of Empire. Mr. Harold Smith M.P. talked about "putting every Liberal woman in their pockets." For a party which can monopolise an Empire the task should be easy.

1 Alderman Bolton earned fame on Tuesday by describing the Insurance Act as "the biggest swindle of this century," and then confessing in the next minute that he "knew nothing about it." Few men have the nerve to make such a strange double-barrelled pronouncement in public, but the Alderman is a law unto himself. His committee had done something without consulting him, and naturally there had to be a scapegoat. The Insurance Act came quite handy. * There has never been a happier evening at the Liberal Club than that of Tuesday when the Liberal Women assembled in their hundreds to make substantial recognition of the brave deed performed by ferryman Samuel Ashworth at Latchford Locks on the night of July 30th. There was the hero, modest and manly, supported loyally by genial skipper Rome and jovial first-officer Mayes. They are a fine trio of typical sea- men and the Liberal women treated them to a spell of hero-worship which was almost embarrassing. There is only one word fully descriptive of the proceedings. Everything passed off with great eclat.

* * It will be remarked that the annual assem- bly of Labour, which opened at Newport on Monday is called in the, official title the "Trades Union "Congress, although com- monly in the agenda the singular form "trade union" is used. The latter, of course, is correct (says the Westminster Gazette). There is no justification at all for the mean- ingless half-plural, although in the early days of labour organisation it was quite accurate. The dream of Robert Owen, first of English Socialists, and of those who caught fire from him, was nothing less than a vast "trades union"—that is, a society consisting of all trades and all workers. But for three-quarters of a century this country has been producing only the "trade union," the one permissable plural of which is "trade unions."

NORD GATED SHEETS and Roofing Felt. Estimates S. GARLAND, Ironmonger, 51, Bridge at.,

The late Mr Thomas Longdin, who would have been sixty-six years of age in November, was born and educated in Manchester, his father being the head of one of the oldest firms of constructional iron f tunders in that city. He entered the service of Mr J Wolstencrolt, architect and surveyor, a, d on the com- pletion of his articles remained with him as managing clerk, his a,sociation with the firm extending over thirteen years. Dur ng that period msny important works and buildings in Manchester and other towns (including Warrington) were bui't under his super- vision, the more notable of those undertakings in Warrington including Messrs Cunliffs's Tannery, Howley; Mr J T Cunliffe's bons*, Walton (now pulled down) and extensions to Messrs Nelson's Tannery, Mer ey-strest, Tanner's-lane Tanning Company (then Mr II Draper's) and the late Mr. R. W. Draper's Tannery, Lymm. Constant contact with the municipalities of Manchester and Salford gave him a thorough insight into the operations of the engineering ssction of corpor- ate work. In 1870 he obtained a Queen's prize, in connection with the Science Classes of the Science and Arts Department. Having attracted the notice of the late Mr. R. Johnston, contractor and builder, of Man- chester, he was induced to join him as confidential managing clerk, and afterwards joined in the same capacity Messrs. Collin and Son, of Warrington, to acquire a practical knowledge of his profession. Lary railway, drainage, and other works were carried out during the period he held the above positions, and the experience thus gained proved invaluable in his sub- sequent official appointments. In 1873 he secured the position of Surveyor to the Maldon Council in Essex, and designed and carried out several drainage and water supply schemes and other work incidental to the office. In January, 1878, he was unanimously appointed Borough fligineer and Surveyor to the Warrington Corporation, and since that time the growth of the borough has increased the work of the department, in regard to the construction of new streets and buildings, to an extent of which the general public can have but small conception. Mr. Longdin had, of course, the designing and supervision of many undertakings of great importance and his skill found general recognition in municipal circles. The last notable work of which be had charge was the erection ' of the footbridge over the Mersey at Howley. Mr. ' Longdin had a great reputation as an expert witness in the promotion of Parliamentary Bills and had also ' rendered conspicuous service in that capacity ; in actions brought by the Corporation. On one cccasion— during the action brought by the Corporation against the Manchester Ship Conal Com- pany—he was in the Witness box for three days and omplimented on the excellent way in w e his evidence. e underwent a long whasichhi he highly c, H ' cross-examination by several eminent K.C's —two of whom are now members of the judicial Bench—and his testimony was of great value to the Corporation. P Air Longtirn leaves a widow, three sons and three daughters. One of his sons is a surveyor in London, another holds a similar position in India, and a third is a mechanical engineer in North Wales. THE FUNERAL.

_ The funeral will take place at Hill Cliffe graveyard this (Saturday) afternoon at two o'clock. The members of the Council and officials will meet at the Town Hall (Central Gates) and await the arrival of the cortege which will leave the late residence of the deceased at 12.45. At one o'clock a service will be held in Holy Trinity Church, after which the proces- sion will be re-formed and proceed to Hill Cliff° via Chester-road. The order will be as follows :- 1 Detachment of Police. 2 Fire Brigade. 3 Deputations from Local Bodies. 4 Townspeople. 5 Mayor, Deputy Mayor and Town Clerk. Town Council and Officials. 7 Workmen of Surveyor's Department. 8 Office Staff of Surveyor's Department. 9 Hearse. 10 Carriages.

The Manchester Tradesmen had won the championship shield for which there were 222 com- petitors with an average weight of 11b. soz. ldr. and each of the six members of the team received gold medals. The members of the Black Bear team who came second with an average weight of 11b. 2oz. 11drs , each received a gold centre medal. J Newton's team came third with 11b. 2oz. 12drs. SUDDEN DEATH OF MR. T. LONGDIN.

A painful sensation was created in Warrington n Wednesday morning by the death, which occurred with tragic suddenness, of Mr. Thomas Longdin, borough engineer and surveyor. For some time Mr. Longdin had been under the medical treatment of Dr. Joseph, Ilaycr of the Borough, but the state of his health was not such as to give rise to any alarm, and he continued to attend to his duties as usual. On Tuesday evening he attended the monthly meeting of the Town Council, and appeared to be m his customary cheerful spirits. He went to his office at the Town Hall on Wednesday morning and at about 10.30 was opening his letters. The deputy town clerk (Mr A Hallaway), had occasion to se him cm municipal business and Mr. Longdin was sitting in his chair, engaged in conversation, when he suddeoly fell forward and drorpel his spectacles. No:icing a stirtling change in his condition, Mr Hallaway immediately rendered assistance and medical aid was also summoned. Dr. Langdale and Dr. Hibbett, the 'medical officer of health, were quickly on the scene, but before their arlival Mr. Longdin had passed away, heart failure being the cause of death. The news of Mr Longdin's unexpected end came as a great shock to his many friends, by whom he was held in high esteem, and deep sympathy has been expressed with the widow and family in their bereavement.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Griffiths—Simcock.

A pretty wedding took place at Grappenhall Church on Tuesday when Mr. Llewellyn Griffiths, son of Mrs. J. R. Griffiths, of Latchford, was married to Miss Ethel Sitncock, of Grappenhall. The Rev. H. V. Pigot officiated. The bride was given away by her father and was attended by Mrs. Maude Lowe. Mr. Godfrey Riley Griffiths, of Franklin, U.S.A., brother of the bridegroom, acted as best man. After a wedding breakfast at the house of Mr. George Simcock, the happy couple departed for North \Vales, amid the good wishes of numerous friends and relatives.

Williams—Lloyd Jones.

' A pretty wedding, which aroused considerable interest, took plaee at the. Baptist Church, Latchford, on Thursday, when Mr. Edward Cyril Williams, son of Mr. Edward Williams, of Birmingham, was married to Miss Mvfanwy Lloyd Jones, second daughter of Mr. and Mrs. t L'oy d Jones, Cartrefle, Latchford Without The Pastor (Rev. J. S. Chedburn) officiated. Mr. Peter Harrison was at the organ and played festal music. The bride, who was given away by her father. was charmingly attired in a gown of ivory taffeta, with underdress of charmeuse, and corsage of silk lace with a veil of embroidered Brussels net, caught with clusters of orange blossoms and white heather. She carried a bouquet of choice white flowers. The bride travelled in a tailor-made costume of navy serge, with hat of cinnamon brown, trimmed with black velvet. Many handsome presents were received.

Wilde—Brown. On Saturday the marriage of Miss Beatrice Brown to Mr. J. H. Wilde was solemnized in the Primitive Methodist Church, Legh-street, by the Rev. Wm. Cooper. The bride and bridegroom are members of the choir of the above church, and are held in the highest esteem by a wide cit cle of friends. Conse- quently a numerous company assembled to witness the ceremony. The bride, who was given away by her brother (Mr. Samuel Brown), was attired in a gown of cream cashmere, trimmed with Irish point lace and silk, a pretty wreath and veil, and carried a bouquet of roses, white heather and chrysanthemums. Her ornament was a gold brooch, the gift of the bride- groom. The bridesmaid was Miss Amy Brown (sister of the bride), who wore a Wedgowood blue dress trimmed with Oriental lace and silk, and a black chip hat adorned with white wing and velvet. Her bouquet was composed of pink carnations, and she wore a gild signet ring, both gifts of the bridegroom. The service was fully choral, Mr. F. Banner officiating at the organ. The newly-married couple received quite an ovation when they emerged from the church. The best man was Mr. A. Wilde, of Westbury, (brother of the bridegroom). The breakfast was served at Atkinson's Cafe. The health of the happy pair was suitably proposed by Rev W Cooper and Mr A Spruce, choirmaster. Attended by the hearty good wishes of their friends, Mr and Mrs Wilde set out for Morecambe where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was navy blue with black velvet hat underlined with tegal. A most enjoyable evening was spent by the wedding guests. The bride and bride- groom have been the recipients of many beautiful presents.

TRADES CONGRESS AND EDUCATION. At Wednesday's sitting of the Trade Unions Congress, meeting in the Central Hall, at Newport, two highly- contr9versial subjects were discussed. On the education question, it was oecided by a majority that the question of secular education be ruled out from the discussions of future congresses. The weakness of the decision is that future congresses may refuse to submit to this limitatioh. With the shadow of Government legis- lation for arbitration in trade disputes overhanging the trade union movement, Mr. Ben Tillett's annual and oft-repeated proposal assumed a more immediate importance. The most interesting thing in the dis- cussion was the conversion of Mr. Havelock Wilson to compulsory arbitration as a result of his visit to Austraha.

, BOY SHOPBREAKERS.

EXTRAORDINARY SERIES OF THEFTS.

" Picture Palaces are to blame ; the boys have seen ridiculous things sometimes shown of persons stealing and running away, and the idea arose from that." This statement was made at the Borough Police Court on Thursday by Mr. Browne, who was defending one of the boy shopbreakers who appeared before Mr. J. Rymer Young (in the chair) and other magistrates. Several charges of breaking into and entering shops and steal- ing money and goods were preferred against H.rold Allen (15), 23, Elizabeth-street ; Frank Cregan (15), 9 Elizabeth-street ; and John William Cook (15), 68, Orford-street, Warrington. A story of criminality of the highest degree was told by Inspector Williamson who said that from the middle of June the police began to receive quite a number of complaints of shops haying been broken into at night. The boys Allen and Cook appeared to have commenced on the 19th June with breaking into the shop of Mr Williams, 61, and 66, Sankey-street. The doors had been forced and, from all appearances, the instrument used was a" jemmy." The till was broken open and £1 12s in money was stolen. The whole thing was set about in a most businesslike and burglar-like manner. The King's Cafe bad been entered by the prisoners. The window had been opened and the catch put back. Collecting boxes for the Waifs and Strays and for another society were broken open and tis was taken. The next case was on July 31st, breaking into the workroom of Miss Hall, Bola-street. The place was thoroughly ransacked and a gold watch was taken from the room. Allen offered it in pawn but the pawn-broker would not take it from him and his aunt got money advanced on it. On the 14th August the premises of Edward Jones, Priestley-street, were broke into by means of the back window which had been broken in the top sash and one of the boys had got through. No money was found but six shirts were taken. Theo on the 29th August the shop of Nellie Light,3, Suez-street was entered through the fan-light and 12s in money was taken and a quantity of chocolate value in alllss. In this case all the three Prisoners were involved. It was quite evident to the police that the same persons had been working in all those cases but it was not until the last case that the prisoners were found out. As a result the boy Cook, who was an errand boy, was interviewed and at first denied having been near the shop but afterwrrds said he went with Cregan and Allen. When charged Allen said "I only took as 7.3 d," Cook said' Myself and Oregan kept watch outside." Cregan replied "I kept watch with Cook and when Allen came out he gave me *1 and a bar of chocolate. Allen went through the fanligat. " Subsequently they were charged with the other offences and raid the money from Mr William's shop was Ll 8s not £1 12s. In that case and the King's Cafe, Cook and Allen alone were charged and they were connected with the theft from liold-street.Alleu bad repled that the watch was not gold but brass. In regard to the offence at Priestley- street Allen said he had sold the shirts for ls 61 to a woman. Cook said he kept watch outside. In conclu- sion the chief inspector said there was no (question of the offences being solitary ones, or of giving away to isolated temptation. There was crime after crime care- fully planned and carried out. Mr Browne defended Allen and Mr Steal appeared for Cook whilst a relative answered for Cregan. All prisoners pleaded guilty. Mr Browne said Allen was the chief support of his family and earned 12s. per week. When the boy was asked about the terrible things, he could only say the picture palaces were to blame. They were to blame. The boys had seen ridiculous things sometimes shown of persons stealing and running away and the idea arose from seeing those pictures. He was the first in the family to appear in court. After quoting the case of a prisoner Tracey, who had been before the Bench that morning and bad first of all been sent to a reformatory, Mr. Browne said the reformatory was absolutely useless. It was the cause, and had been the cause of creating a continuous criminal career after the first paltry offence, lie was not calling those paltry offences but he did not want the lad to be sent to a reformatory and the prison was getting out-of-date in regard to youths, end he asked that they should be bound over. The money had been spent in playing cards and amusements.

Mr Steel siid 030 k found himself in that position owing to the influence of the boy Allen. They were both employed at the same place and Cook had told him that the suggestion of Mr Williams' shop came from Allen. Cook had never gone in the shops but bad stayed outside. It appeared to him that Allen had been the boy with the master mind and Cook had had practically nothing of what had been taken. The relative who appeared for Cregan said the boy had been left in his charge by the father from whom he bad not heard for some time. The Chief Constable said he felt he ought to say something in regard to reformatories. He did not agree with what Mr Browne had said, for he knew cases where boys who had been sent there had turned out r respectable citizens. Those were typical cases where the boys should be sent to a reformatory. Apart from the charges which had been preferred against them they could have preferred many charges of conspiracy. From Mr Steel it would appear that the boy he was defend- ing was an angel in disguise.—Mr Steel "I don't want that." The Chief Constable: That is what I thought. I refute the statements of Mr Browne re reformatory schools.

The boys were committed to a reformatory for four year&

THE LATE MRS. GARNETT.

THE FUNERAL.

By the death of Mrs. Robert Garnett, of Hall Nook, Penketh, not only have the via ions organisations and institutions in which she took an active interest suffered a great loss, but she will be much missed by the general public in the village with which she had been sl long identified. At the funeral at St. Mary's Church, Great Sankey, on Monday there were many , evidences of the great respect entertained for Mrs. Garnett, and it was plainly apparent from the large number of villagers who attended the services, that ' she had endeared herself to the hearts of all. There was a large congregation at a memorial service in the Penketh Wesleyan Church, prior ti the interment. The communion rails and pulpit were covered with mourning draperies, and as the principal mourners assembled, the Dead March was played on the organ by Mr. Clatworthy. The Rev John Gunnell conducted the service and he was assisted by the Rev W A Beckett. In addition to the family mourners there were present :—Mr F A Harris (circuit steward, Warrington): Mr James Watson (society steward Penketh); Mr Hampson (representing Penketh and Sankey Cottage Garden Association); Mr J C Holland (repiesenting the Warrington Auxiliary of the Bible Society); Mrs Simpson, Mrs Tanner, and Mrs George Dale (representing the British Women's Temperance Association) ; a deputation from the Warrington Women's Liberal Association, Mr and Mrs Arthur Scott, Mr John Allen, Mr and Mrs .1 T Ambler, Mrs Gunnell, Mr and Mrs J Atherton, Mr Joseph Whitmore, Alderman Bennett,Mr T S Riley, Mr John Randall, Miss Bessie Dutton, the Rev John and Mrs Yonge, Mr S W Fildes, Mr and Mrs Waterhouse, Mrs Benson, Mr and Mrs Buckley, Mr Ernest Garnett, Mr and Mrs Wm. Boardman, Mrs J Wright, Mis P Wright, Mr C H Mitchell (Liverpool), Councillor Skelland, Councillor Birtles, Mr F Taylor, Dr Murray, the Rev E Morgan, the Rev Win Reid.and the Rev H Williamson. Tile congregation joined in the singing of the hymns "0 God our help in ages past" and "Rock of ages cleft for me." The cort6ge then proceeded to Sankey Church where a large number of the residents of Sankey had assembled to pay a last tribute to the dead. The colEn was borne on an open conveyance, draped iu purple and covered with many beautiful floral tributes. The Rev J Ii Jones (Vicar) conducted the service, assisted by the Rev John Gunnell, who also performed the last rites at the graveside. The choir was in attendance, and sang the hymn " Abide with Me." Miss Standish presided at the organ and played " 0 rest in the Lord." Many beautiful wreaths were placed on the grave, amongst those sending floral tributes being The Girls' Club, Warrington : Sewing Room and Employees of R Garnett and Sons, Warrington ; The Staff, Bold-street, Liverpool ; Warrington Women's Liberal Association; Employees, Penketh Lodge ; Chairman and Committee of the Penketh and Sankey Cottage Garden Association ; and British Women's Temperance Association.
