MARY GARNETT-AN APPRECIATION.

There is usually a natural shrinkage of life's activities in the case of those whose earthly journey has exceeded the alloted span of three score years and ten. Especially is this so where death has taken from men or women their life's companion. With Mary Garnett there was nothing of this sort. As a friend writes: "I am sure you must feel a sense of comfort in one respect ; you knew no decline of her powers, she never became the suggestion of her former self. So you can think of her as you always knew her, the bright keen intellect showing no abatement, the mind undimmed, the spirit unbroken." As the years increased and the physique became feebler there was a continuous expanston of the true life, and that to the very end. She seemed to have acquired that rarest of attainments the art of growing old, and at the same time remaining young, happily aLd gracefully. In heart she never grew old, her heart, her true self, was I ever young. At nearly four score years she retained her freshness and mental vigour. Nothing could daunt or quench her spirit ; she grew freer with I added years, age had no influence on her mind or heart. An illustration of this vitality is shown by the fact that Mrs. Garnett had arranged this week to go to Italy along with her son and a party of friends, and had her life been prolonged she would now have been on her way to the Sunny South. She was looking forward with great zest to the prospect of renewing her associations with that wonderfully interesting country she knew so well. She had remarked to several friends during the last few weeks " I shall take my flight with the swallows to a summer clime "—and she has taken it. Members of her family were hoping to receive from Italy more of those delightful letters which had come to them during former trips. These letters were written with no thought of publication, written at odd moments, often under the strain and amidst the turmoil of journeying when most travellers make picture postcards serve for home letters. These letters, racy and unconventional, but exceedingly graphic, revealed wide knowledge and keen observa- tion. On reading those printed some years ago for private circulation along with some of her husband's sketches, one regrets that Mrs. Garnett did not embody her recollections and thoughts in a form accessible to a wider audience. Perhaps she was restrained by her own high standard of what was worthy of publication. But it was not necessary for Mrs. Garnett to visit other lands or to write letters to be interesting. Those who were privileged to hear her talk, hear her describe some apparently common-place human incident, will never forget the graphic touches, the lively gestures, the vivid visualization, the humour that ever bubbled up. Amongst the members of her own family, with all their different characteristics she found a mine of interest and a good deal of fun. She laughed with them and at them all. She never missed the fun, and it was good to see her laugh, not in her head alone, but with her whole being. One of her family says it was better to hear " Grannie " describe a scene than to witness it ; she often saw far more in it than those present. Some might think she saw more than there was to see; not a bit, she simply had the eyes to see and the genius to express what unobserved was there the whole time. There was nothing small about Mary Garnett, she was big in body, in mind, and in soul. She loved stately cathedrals, vast spaces. big trees, strong men with large endeavour, efficient women with power and purpose. Strength, endurance, whole- ness, were the things which appealed to her. Instinctively she drew away from weakness and defect in everything, and it was only her love for her kind, and sympathy for others that enabled her to hold in check this instinctive feeling when feeble- ness called for her strength and suffering for her succour. This love for what was perfect, strong and enduring drew her to the contemplation of the monu- mental works of men of past ages, which abound in countries like Italy, and also to the study cf standard literature of the great men and women of all ages and all lauds in which she revelled. _

Mrs. Garnett possessed all the grace and charm of womanhood. These were united with masculine vigour, ripe judgment, rare native wit, and sound common- sense. Without claiming for Mary Garnett immunity from the faults common to our human nature, taking her all in all, she was one of nature's noblest, and thinking of her personality, character, and gifts, we feel it will be long before we look upon her like again. C. & H. P.

THE LATE MRS. GARNET?.

I cannot intrude on the " Examiner" and its readers what the rush of tender and cherished memories clustering round the rare and beautiful friendship of forty years suggests. I know besides how much of the best of the life now closed was given to our town and its surroundings by one who never asked "Who is my neighbour?" and whose devotion to public work—religious and social—broadened out to all churches and to all that was for God and the people. The mourning band of workers, lay and clerical, who like myself felt a new enthusiasm when they heard her inspiring words, and marked the indomitable spirit in which she enaured weariaess and disregarded opposition, feel that now their labour will lack an inspiration they would give much to recall. Of the private helps, kindnesses, and sympathies kpown to few indeed but the recipients, she would not have us speak. What a treasure of rare and intellectual qualities was in her social intercourse. Her genial capibility of adapting herself to varieties of " folk " was the way to the attainment of the greatest joy of her lite—the possession of troops of frienos. Her unreserved and sympathetic neighbourliness to all, high and low, in her own village, was one beauti- ful antidote to the sometime meagreness of village life. Her Opportunities for wide and constant travel during Mr. Garnett's life, and

5

bubsequent3y, Irstre frequent ; and her delight and appreciation, has power to appropriate so largely enjoyment and informtion—caused all she wrote smsf recounted afterwards tot be just what she intended— a huge satisfaction to thtslisteners only second to thal of the sightseer herself. have been her comrade on many a pleasant and extended journey at constant intervals through the course of our long cherished friendship. Those who have once bad her travelling companionship, and been 9beltered by her calm courage and adaptability in all circumstances will, like myself, feel that all the zeal and ardour for any future "tramp abroad "will be subdued by an indescribable regret. This friend who had found an inner place in the hearts of so many, has now gained the eternal joy and rest which was so constantly in life the centre of her glad thought. And all the grief-stricken members of her large family, to whom she was so devoted, and cf her inner circle of friends, can yield her to the rejoicing of union with those she loved best—her Saviour, and her fitA of earth—" gone before." _ . _

" We give thee thanks 0 Lord for all Thy servsnts departed this life in Thy faith and fear." P. D.

Should Harvest Festivals be Held P

Bishop of Chester on Weather Prayers.

A long letter from the Bishop of Chester has been addressed to the rural deans in the Diocese of Ceester, and from it we quote the following :—The persistent rains which have deluged our crops cannot but revive the old question—" In such a year is it wise to hold harvest festivals ? Can we expect farmers to give special thanks when they have so little to be thankful for? With our greatly enlarged knowledge of meteorology, what reason or reality is there in praying for changes of weather ? Is there not sounder piety in accepting submissively sunshine and rain, even when they seem to us unseasonable and excessive, as the all-wise Ruler of the world may think Otto send them?" The thing most needful is, I submit, habitual prayer for seasonable weather. We should be slow to ask for I changes of weather. We Can see but a little way ahead, and the weather that looks now so disastrous may in the long, if not the short run, put on a kindlier aspect. Which of us, if he had the ooeer to-day, would under- take the responsibility of bidding the rain cease? In view, however, of some exceptional impending calamity —a famine, for example, in some part of our Empire—it is natural and right that specially fervent prayer should accompany special efforts to avert or meet the distress, and we may be humbly confident that slice prayers will be found in one way or another to avail much. As regards harvest festivals, have they not for most of us an increased appropriateness in a year of damaged crops ? Should we not be thankful that through friendly commerce between nations the harvests of the world are ours ? that the price of food, though it may rise, will not rise to famine height, as it VC ould if we were dependent upon our own resources alone? that we can already see some compensations in the general good health of the country and in other respects, if we will but "count up our mercies " and weigh them in just balances, instead of exhibiting a pettish and dis- satisfied temper like those who have not yet all they want? Should not our ideas of the range of thanks- giving and the harvest be widened and enriched ? Farmers, we admit, have some excuse, and they claim our true sympathy, which will this autumn be shown, as in other ways, by more harvest festival offertories and others contributions going to aid those suffering members of the farming brotherhood who have been borne down in the battle of life. • But will the farmers be obliged to us for that enervating sort of sympathy which assumes that they will not face their losses and disappointments like men and Christian men? that they are not prepared to take the rough with the smooth ; to receive evil at the hand of God as well as good ; to set compensations fairly against disadvantages; to regard the duty of thankfulness as extending over more than one year's and one country's harvest; to confess "it is good for me that I have been in trouble," not only for the moral and spiritual discipline thus gained, but because weather troubles, the pressure of foreign competition' and other difficulties have snore than once stimulated English agriculture to the adoption of improved ideas, methods, and appliances? Quit ye like men " is one word of respectful sympathy they will value from us. "Be ye thankful "is another ; for the thankful heart is the courageous, enduring, elastic, cheerful heart which goes all the way and all the day. Gloominess, grumbling, and ingratitude never yet mace our troubles less.

ALTERATIONS TO ST. GEORGE'S HALL. KING EDWARD MEMORIAL SCHEME

SANCTIONED AT LIVERPOOL.

By a vote of the Liverpool City Council on Wednes- day a decision seems to have been taken ou a problem which has greatly perturbed the community for many months past. It concerned the future of St. George's- Hall. The"difficulty arose from the need for a site for the statue of King Edward VII., for which a public subscription has already provided the becessary funds. Local opinion has held that the site should be near St. bleorge's Hall, but existing statues occupy the best positions, and to find a site for the late King's monument involved tome modification in the surroundings of the Hall. The project ultimately favoured by the Finance Cora- -1 . mlttee of the Corporation was to cut down the "podium," or plateau, supported by a high buttress wall on the south side of the Hall, and to replace it, by fights of steps, at the summit of which the statue of King Edward should be erected. The proposal was strongly objected to by many. Architectural and artistic opinion, was divided, but the greater force was undoubtedly against the scheme. The Finance Committee, however, held their ground, and were sup- ported by the special committee—of which Lord Derby is chairman—appointed to carry out the memorial. At Wednesday's meeting of the City Council, when the matter came up, there was a h..ated discussion, curtailed, however, by the Lord Mayor's insistence on points of order. In the end the Committee's proposal was carried by 52 votes to 40. Upon the announce- ment of the result an opponent of tbe scheme bitterly suggested that the names of its supporters should be placed on a tablet for the "contempt of future generations."

OLD AGE PENSIONER AS TREE CLIMBER. Ongar Guardians were informed on Tuesday that their relieving officer had given medical relief to an old age pensioner, aged 80, of Moreton, who had been climbing up a plum tree and had fallen down, injuring himself. It wns agreed to sanction the relief and to inform the pensioner that he is too old to climb trees.

The Fire Engine Committee reported, at a meeting held on August 19th. that copies of the returns relating to motor fire engines, prepared by the Borough Surveyor, having been sent to each member of the com- mittee, and after fully considering the same, it was resolved "That the members of the committee, together with the Borough Surveyor and the Superinbendeut, visit St. Helens and Birkenhead for the purpose of inspecting the motor fire engines in use there." Alderman BURTON, in proposing the minutes of the committee, referred to the visit of the members of the committee to St. Helens and Birkenhead to inspect the motor fire engines in use there. The members were most courteously received and obtained some very useful information at the two places. The superin- tendents of the brigades were perfectly satisfied with motor engines, a considerable saving having been effected by their use. He thought the visit had removed altogether any doubts members of the committee had as to the efficiency of motor engines. Councillor ASHCROFT seconded the confirmation of the minutes. He thought every member of the com- mittee was perfectly satisfied that they ought to have a motor engine, and that Warrington was fifty or a hundred years behind. ("No, no.") A motor engine could turn out in five seconds after the alarm, and lives and property might he saved by such promptitude. Councillor SANKEY moved and Councillor WILSON seconded the passing of the minutes. Councillor CRAIK moved as an amendment that the minutes relating to the salaries be referred back. Both Mr Parfltt and Mr Wilson had, he said, received annual increases up to four years ago and were sufficiently well paid for the work they had undertaken. Alderman BOLTON seconded and contended that if a maximum had been reached it should not be exceeded without reasonable cause. If annual increases of £5 were previously thought sufficient, he did not see why an advance of £25 should now ba given. Councillor ASHCROFT supported the amendment and raid be considered it was time the members of' the Council stopped increasing salaries, especially as the rates of the town were so high. Councillor WILSON supported the minutes and said he would like to take the members of the Council back in memory to the condition of things which existed when Messrs Parfltt and Wilson took charge of the Longford works. At one time they were a desert. Nothing was shipshape, and the town had been saved hundreds of pounds by what Mr. Wilson had done. He was a man who gave of his best and he knew gentlemen sitting in the Council Chamber who, if they had to work as an ordinary man outside, and do the work Mr Wilson had to do, would not be worth thirty" bob" a week. (Laughter.) Messrs. Parfltt and Wilson were not getting the pay they ought to get for the work they were doing which had increased with the expansion of the town. Warrington stood quite equal t 3 other towns in the matter of sanitation. Alderman JAMES EVANS said there were exceptional ,circumstances in the case of Messrs Parfltt and Wilson which justified the exceeding of the maximum. There was a striking difference in the condition of the works compared with some years ago, and if it were only for the conditions under which the men worked, their positions were worth considerably more money When the maximum was laid down, it was no doubt considered fair, but they bad two exceptional men, the work in whose departments bad grown to an extent which was not. anticipated when the maximum was fixed. Councillor DICKENSON asked what was the maximum previously fixed? The MAYOR said it was £2OO, and the present recommendations of the committee sought to vary the maximum. Councillor DicKsxsosr: My contention is that it is folly to discuss fixing any maximum when it is to be over-ridden by the committee. The MAYOR: There is plenty of time in eight years to alter one's opinion. Councillor DICKENSON : It does not matter to me if it is 100 years. I say it is no use fixing a maximum if the committee is to over-ride it and give these men an advance of £25. In a few months we shell find that it is proposed to vary someone else's maximum. This sort of thing lags taken place ever since I have been in the Council. I quite agree that these men are well paid. It has been said what a desert the works used to be,but these men have not affected the change ; they have paid servants under them who have done the work. They have supervised it—that is all. The members of the Council are not giving these advances out of their own money ; it belongs to the ratepayers. If we were to go outside and ask the ratepayers whether these advances should be given, I am afraid they would not get any support. Alderman SMETHURST, in supporting the recom- mendations of the committee, said he had been a mem- ber of the Sanitary Works Committee ever since he entered the Council and knew the great change which had taken place in the condition of the works. Messrs. Parfltt and Wilson well deserved the proposed increase —in fact two better men could not be found for the positions, and their work was most important from the 'health point of,view. The Mayon said he was a member of the Sanitary Works Committee. Councillor DICKENSON asked for the names to be recorded in the voting which was about to take place. The MAYOR said that was a matter of supererogation, as the names were usually taken. Councillor lionEstrs said he wished to enter a quiet protest against that implied threat. Every member of the Corporation came to the Council Chamber to do his duty according to his conscience ' • and if he had to pay the penalty of public criticism for doing what he thought to be right, the publication of the names in the voting would not deter any man who was worth his salt doing his duty. What was suggested was absolutely unnecessary and was a vulgar side of public life. (Hear, bear.) After the explanation which had been given by the Mayor, he should vote for the increases of salary. He was prepared to put his faith in tbe Mayor's opinion but what appealed to his mind more than anything else was that the two departments in question were, perhaps, the most important in the whole of the Corporation's administrati% e work. If the work was not thoroughly and efficiently done—and he could speak as a property owner and a ratepayer—an epidemic of sickness might be brought about which would cost the town an incalculable sum. This year Warrington bad reached a record in the matter of its low death-rate, and compared with other towns its sanitary condition was extremely satisfac- tory ; and that state of things to some extent—he would not say entirely—was attributable to the manner in which the duties of the two departments in question had been carried out. (Hear, hear.) They could not, of course, go on indefinitely increasing salaries, but,l as the borough increased in size, and the work of the departments increased, it was only natural and reason- able that those who held the re- ponsible positions of managers should expect consideration in consequence. Whether the committee were quite wise in making one jump of £25 he was cot sure. If they had followed the principle adopted for several years of giving an increase of £lO a year, nobody could have taken excep- tion to it. However, they had not done so, and under the circumstances be felt bound, acting conscientiously, to pay deference to the views expressed by those who knew far better than ho how the work was carried out, and support the minutes. MR. COLERIDGE-TAYLOR.

Mr. Samuel Coleridge-Taylor, the well-known composer, died on tiundav last from acute pneumonia. He was only 37 years of age. Mr. Coleridge-Taylor was an Anglo-Aftican,his father being a Wet African and his mother English. He is credited with .59 productions, his most popular being "Hiawatha," written in 1898.

GAS PROFITS FOR THE RELIEF OF RATES. The accounts passed for payment by the Gas Com- mittee included £5,000 in aid of rates on account..

Alderman TINNION thought there ought to be an explanation of such an important payment, lie was surprised that the finances of the Corporation should be in need of £5,000 from gas profits at such an early stage. Alderman BOLTON said it was the first time in his experience that they had had to hand over £5,000 in anticipation of profits. The TOWN CLERK: It is last year's profits. Alderman BOLTON : I understood you got last year's profits when the balance sheet was made op. To my mind, this is irregular. There is a proper time for collecting money and receiving it. Councillor ROBERTS congratulated Alderman Tinnion on his successful attempt to mystify the Council and suggested that he bad missed his vocation. The fact was that the Gas Committee made a very satisfactory profit last year and decided to hand over £ll,OOO to the rates. The £5,000 in question was simply a payment on account. It was not an anticipation of next year's profits but a payment out of last year's. The minutes were approved. HOWLEY FOOTBRIDGE.

A WARNING.

Alderman SMETH URST, in proposing the confirmation of the minutes of the Bridges Committee, said com- plaints bad been made of young men and lads standing on the Howley Bridge and swinging it. In one case he knew of an old couple who were going over the bridge, and were thrown against the side by the swinging of the bridge by between twenty and thirty youths. It should be generally known that people were not allowed to swing the bridge, and that it was a very dangerous practice. The vibration must affect the iron The MAYOR : The police have instructions with regard to it. Alderman SMETHURST said be bad also heard of young fellows pulling brancees off the trees in the plantations. If the police had instructions about the matter he hoped they would catch some of the youths. There was no trouble in catching them ; he could have caught some himself. The MAYox, in seconding tho confirmation of the mintues, said he corroborated all Alderman Smethurst had said. He hoped the pernicious practice would cease.

Councillor AnCITErt said he did not think the approach to any bridge, within many miles of Warring- ton was in such a disgraceful state as the approach to • Howley Bridge. There were many people who did not know the way to it. The approach was in a chronic state of disorder, and he hoped the Chairman of the committee would recommend the carrying out of a really substantial scheme of improvement. If he did so he would earn the respect of all the burgesses in the district. (Hear, hear). The minutes were confirmed. RE NR Limas ON VEHICLES. THE MAKING OF NEW STREETS. Alderman fixxiox before Alderman Evans rose to submit the motion, asked for it to be withdrawn. The Paving and Sewerage Committee met on the previous evening, and it was thought that such a matter should not be dealt with by resolution. The matter was a very important one, and circumstances existed which might be prejudiced if a discussion took place that night. Lie therefore asked Alderman Evans, on behalf

of the Paring and Sewerage Committee to withdraw the motion, as it was not desirable that the matter should be dealt with by resolution. .

Alderman JAMES EVANS said he had no desire to hamper the work of the Paving and Sewerage Com- mittee, and after what Alderman Tinnion had told him, he did not think the time was opportune to present the resolution. He would withdraw it on the understand- ing that that committee would seriously consider the question which was very important from more aspects than one. (Hear, hear.) The Council then rose.

OPEN-AIR POLITICS.

TORY TACTICS EXPOSED.

Another well-attended meeting under the ausp•ces of the Progressive Labour League, was held at Bridge Foot on Monday evening. Mr. E. James presided, and the chief speaker was Councillor Grindle, of Manchester.—The Chairman drew a comparison between the Tory party and the Liberal party, and he soon convinced his audience that whereas one was inclined to be sleepy and dormant where the workers' interests were concerned, the other was awake and always progressive. The Councillor devoted himself almost entirely to the Insurance Act and his explanation of the beneficent measure one of the best ever heard at Bridge Foot. The CHAIRMAN said that when he came to read and consider what the present Government had dope since they were returned to power in 1906 he was satisfied that, considering the opposition they had had to con- tend with, they had done wonderful work indeed. (Hear' hear.) The Tory ratty had passed measures which hent.fitted the classes, but when the Liberal Government came into office it soon got to work to pass measures which would be for the benefit of the masses.' That was just the difference between the two parties. Previous to 1906 the Tories had continually made promises to the people, but they never kept those promise; and the country turned them out of of3ce and put in power a Government whose sole aim and desire wai to do something for the alleviation ot the suffering masses. Old Age Pensions had been promised for a long time by the Tory Party but it was not until the Liberal Government came into power that any- thing tangible was done for the benefit of our aged people. (Hear, hear). The Tories even fought against that measure and then they wanted to pass off as friends of the working classes. When Mr. Lloyd George introduced his Budget one leading Tory said " The only objection I have to it, is I that it pulls too much on the well to do, and not enough on the workingclasses."Reciently there had been a by-election in Crewe and the Tory party issued pamphlets which said with regard to the workingmen's children going to school : "The workingman's child ought to leave school when he or she is 11 years of age. ("Shame.") That is plenty long enough for tt workingman's child to go to school. He or she can get all they will want to know up to 11 years of age." It meant that the Tory party were convinced that the workingclass of the country,through the education they had received,were beginning to think and study for them- selves. They had found out that the party who had passed off as their friends were their bitterest enemies. The Liberal Party had not only helped the old people but they were working for the betterment of the conditions of the whole working class population of the country, and now they were doing something for both young and old. (Hear' hear.) The Insurance Act had been placed on the Statute Book by the Liberals and they were proud of their achievements. (Applause) Whatever the people might find fault with in the side issues of the Party, if there was nothing else the Government deserved the support of the workingman for, it was that beneficent Act they had passed. When the Act had been in operation a short time and the people were receiving the great benefits, those who to-day were ridiculing and trying to pull the measure to pieces would have a different tale to tell to the Tory Party who were doing all they could to wreck the Act. (Hear, hear.) In supporting the Liberal Party he was convinced that he was supporting a party that was doing work which was right and just for the masses of the people. (Applause.) Councillor GRINDLE began his speech by a reference to the recent by-elections and said the people who were complaining and crow- ing about the by-elections forgot the comparison between the Liberal party and the Tory party. When they threw up the reins of office in 1906. The Tories had lobt not seven by-elections but 20. Not only that, but 11 Tory members of the House of Commons had gone over to the other side which meant a total loss of 31 seats. When the Tories went into power in 1900 they had a majority of 134 and they stuck to the emoluments of office until their majority had gone down to one of 74. The position of the Liberal party was a long way from that. But when the 15th of January came and the people were enjoying the benefits of the Insurance Act, the tide would turn. Gratitude in politics was, however, a quantity not often heard oh. Be often wondered where was the gratitude of the millions of people who were receiving the old age pension ? He believed that by that measure .£13,000,000 was being theput innetco, thesar necessaries of fs lifeof paenodp lteh e re whow was n wereo betterspendingy o wai t on f giving impetus to trade than that. (Hear, hear). If the money were spent on luxuries it would not be half as beneficial to trade, as on the necessities of life. The position of the Liberal party was a strong one. The programme of the Tory party was very weak. The only item of any importance in their programme was Tariff Reform. (Laughter.) When Joseph Chamberlain started the Tariff Reform propaganda in 1903 he said It was then or never for Tariff Reform. At every by-election in that year it was shown that Tariff Reform was not wanted. Then again at all the elections since then it had been "now or never," but the people had shown that it was not yet and never likely to be. (hear, hear). It had been stated that

NoRTH-wEST MANCHESTER was a Tariff Reform victory, but It was not. Sir John Randles' own supporters had shown themselves to be against Tariff Reform. (Hear, hear.) The people should take no notice whatever of the Tory lies. Speaking on Saturday at Cockermouth, Mr. Wyndham made the same mistake he did on Blackpool sands six weeks ego. That error had been pointed out but he still made the same statements. lie said that Canada produced last year 475,000,000 bushels of wheat and we only required last year 271,000.000 bushels. Lie then said Canada could easily supply all we want without any other country coming in at all. Froin his figures it would appear we could, but the mistake was that Mr. Wyndham had added wheat and oats together. The fact was that so far Its wheat was concerned it was 215,000,000 bushels. Of course that was only a slight error of about 120 per cent. (Laughter.) But the people who attended those meetings at which the statements were made were not in a position to correct them. He had added wheat and oats together and thrown it at his audience and so con- veyed an absolutely fallacious idea. The speaker then dealt at some length with the Insurance Act and said the main question with many of the objectors to the Act was whether the scheme was going to touch their pockets. The people who paid least said most and vice versa. Mr. Vickers, of the firm of Vickers, Son and

and Maxim, who was tbe Conservative candidate at Sheffield at the last election, speaking at an opening celemony at a Conservative Club at Sheffield this year said" I hove you all agree with ma in ..

. . _ WELCOMING THE INSURANCE BILL." The hair of the people who constituted the audience stood on end, and Mr. Vickers continued " I don't think it is a perfect Bill, bot it is a splendid beginning. In my own firm we employ 12,000 bands at Sheffield and 15.000 at Ba• Tow." He therefore knew the cost of the Bill, but those who had to pay a great deal did not object. The people should not be misled by the reports in some of the daily papers but study Acts of Parliament in a proper manner and then they would be able to put their own construction on things. In prov- ing how the %corkers got ninepennyworth of insurance for considerably less than fourpence, the speaker said I that Mr. Lloyd George had provided under the Act that the low-waged worker would get his insurance for less than the better paid employees. Giving many figures of the wages, and benefits under the Act, the speaker thoroughly convinced his audience that they would get lin reality ninepence for fourpence. A comparison was drawn between the old system of insurance and the advance and advantage in the new National Insurance scheme. Continuing, Councillor Grindle said that when Ipeople were comparing the advantages of the old system with those of the new, they did not consider everything Some people complained that they were! never ill and yet they had to pay their fourpences for the benefit of others. Those people ought to be thank- ful they were never ill. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Lloyd George believed in making the broadest hacks bear the biggest burdens. People who talked in a selfish way forgot what insurance was. Some people had the idea that insurance was a system by which they paid a few coppers and then after a certain time started drawing out 10s per week. Insurance was an equalisation of burdens and if a person had no burdens he should I contribute to those who had. The Act was going to make a healthier nation. (Hear, hear). It was going to lessen the sickness of the working class. Some time ago a Poor Law Commission issued a report to the I effect that nearly half the pauperism of our country was due to sickness. The Act was going to deal with sick- ness in different ways. First of all it was going to tackle consumption. Parliament had voted £1,500,000 of public money for the provision of sanatoria and as Mr Lloyd George had said, it was like having a line of forts from one end of the country to the other. They were not forts to take life but PORTS TO SAVE LIFE, which was far better. (Hear, bear.) There were 1,500 people dying every week of that fell disease. The medical profession who had fought so successfully against small pox had said that consumption could be stamped out, and Mr Lloyd George had made a step in the right direction. When the Titanic went down 1,500 I lives were lost. The world was horror stricken and a gigantic subscription was got up. Quite that number were going down every week into a premature grave, owing to consumption, and yet nothing very prac.ical had been done before the Insurance Act, to stamp gut the disease. They had made a beginning and that was the most important part. I Then there was the maternity benefit, so dear to the! mothers of the country. (Hear, bear.) In conclusion the speaker said the men who had brought the Act forward were practical men. Mr. Lloyd George bad I been brought up in a cottage home. He knew where poverty pinched and knew just what the people wanted to help them. (Applause.) The Liberal Government had dope much in the past but their programme for the future was a solid one and they tully deserved the support of the working class of the country for all they! had done and were doing for their benefit. (Applause) Mr. R. OSBORNE, in proposing a vote of thanks, dealt , with the efforts of the Tory Party to stay the operation of the Act. Mr. Boner Law had said the Act would never come into operation. In spite of all the objec- tions, the Act was now working tmoothly, and in the I next 12 months the peaple would be receiving the benefits and the Liberal Party would get their whole hearted support. In regard to the servant tax they had heard so much about, Mr. Lloyd George proved that SO per cent. of the Cases in the London Hospitals were domestic servants. They had a good party and a good policy and they should support it with all their heart. (Applause). Mr. JAMES seconded and the. vote was heartily given.

CHESHIRE EDUCATION.

Open-air Classes Prescribed.

At the meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee at Crewe, on Monday, the Elementary Sc'ools Sub- Committee recommended the adoption of suggestions made by the county medical officer, which included the following :—" That head teachers should be encouraged to carry on open-air classes in suitable weather ; that iniall cases children who are about to receive swimming instruction should, prior to commencing such instruc- tion, be medically examined. " 't he recommendations were adopted. Miss C A Hart, of Paisley, KU appointed physical drill inspector and instructor in girls and rnix.d elementary schools in the county at a salary of £l2O a year, and Mr. F. Mills, of Guildford, received a similar appointment for the boys' schools at a salary of £l5O a year. UNIVERSITY SCHOLARSHIPS. It was reported that University scholarships had been awarded to the following :—Philip G Kemp, Mauls (Cambridge University) ; E Brewerton, Wilmslow (Oxford University) ; Leonard J Dooley, Macclesfield (Cambridge) ; G W Cleve, klac3lestield (Cambridge) ; Phillip Heap, Crewe (Manchester University) ; James Poole, Crewe (Manchester); Edith Broadbent, Hyde (Manchester) ; Ada Bristow, Northenden (Manchester) ; Roth Hemingway, Wallasey (Liverpool University). The" Bibby" Scholarship, tenable for three years at Liverpool University, has been awarded to Dora Gandy of Runcorn Secondary School. Two county techno- logical scholarships were awarded to Percy Morrey, Crewe, and John Albinson, of Macclesfield ;and a music scholarship was awarded to Frank Tipping. Agricultural scholarships, tenable at the Cheshire Agricultural and Horticultural College at Holmes Chapel, were awarded to C L Foams, H M Dodd, J Richardson, W R Foulkes, and lan Robertson.

TRIBUTE TO DEAD BARONET.

Alderman Hodgson referred to the death of Sir Baker Wilbraham, vice-chairman of the Education Committee, and said that Cheshire was much poorer for his loss. lie had laid the foundations for Important agricultural movements, and was the chairman of the Cheshire Agricultural College. The county ow!,d much to his valuable advice, sympathy, and enthusiasm in times of difficulty. The Rev J Grant Bird also expressed appreciation of Sir Baker's work.

THROUGH SIEGE OF LUCKNOW.

At the age of 81, Surgeon-Major Bernard Kendal, who, with his wife and childron, underwent the perils of the siege of Lucknow, died at Upper Norwood on Tuesday.

_ From Reynolds Newspaper.

A WINTER BETTER THAN SUMMER.

Who does not feel that the summer of 1912 has been a fraud ? Who does not feel that in some quarter of the globe there should he compensation, where nature will make recompense for the ingraciou.ness of the year and give smiles and sun instead of frowns and gloom ? We are all tired of reading of abnormal rain- fall, of ruined harvests, of statistics which tell us of sunless days and what they ought to be. We decline too, to be consoled by weather prophets who could charm us with pictures of a brilliant autumn. Sep- tember may offer some compensation but the shadow and chill of October is upon us and we shudder at the thought of November and December days in England. " Days "do not adequately describe the few hours of watery daylight, the longer ones of gloom and the longest of dark nights. What a contrast autumn and winter in the Mediter- ranean offer to all this ! How much more healthy and pleasant to enjoy a November and December in countries where you find a shade temperature of 50 degrees or so and winter sunshine of SO or 90 degrees. To visit the Cote d'Azur once is the surest pledge that you will go again, go every winter. Nowhere else will be found such gaiety, such feast of colour, such sumptuous life, such gay society. Here too you will see the world's scenery at a alance, Arctic to the North, tropical to the South ; snowclad Alps at your back, the gleaming Mediterranean at your feet. A foretaste of the luxury and delight of travel is enshrined in those three magio letters " P.L.M." On the five or six thousand miles of this magnificent system from the portal of Paris you travel by many routes to the pleasures of the Riviera. By Vichey and Clermont, by the dual lines of the valley of the Rhone. access to the pageantry of the coast lines from Nimes to Italy is made more pleasant for the travellers. Trains de luxe of the most luxurious rolling stool; in the world make travelling ideal in its comfort. The sleeping saloons with their well equipped night corn torts, their luxurious dressing rooms, prevent all thought of weari- ness. The traveller is taken across a Continent with less fatigue than the two or three hours of a local line and its wearisome waits in England, and almost regrets the end of the journey. Apart from the well-planned main lines of travel, the ' P.L.M. show rare enterprise in linking up some of the finest country and scenery by luxurious motor services. Some of these are for summer travel only and their routes close with the mid-September. Others,however, offer many a pleasant inland trip, or exploration of beauty spots along the coast not touched by the train service. One popular delusion in regard to the Riviera is that its pleasures are confined to the world of fashion and the invalid, that their limit is the promenade at Nice, the Casino at Monte Carlo and the sheltered life of Mentone. Winter sports are easy of access from every point, the skifields and the delights of the bob-sleigh are not confined to Switzerland. Quite hear to Nice will be found an admirable sport centre at Peira-Cava. To much more' sedate minds, the student and antiquarian, the Riviera offers a rich field. The earliest, records of humanity are found near Monaco and nowhere can be found finer relics of " the grandeur that was Rome" than in the 1 lower valley of the Rhone, at Valence, Orange, Nimes, Arles and elsewhere. To those who enjoy sea travel, the ports of Northern Africa are less than a day's Journey, and many a quaint spot in Algeria and Tunis may be visited from Oran on the West to Sfax in the East. Altogether, for winter pleasure, you can never go to the wrong quarter if you travel by the P.L.M. CHARLES KNOTT.

The Examiner, Saturday, September 7, 1912.

The expense of these improvements had been raised by levies on the people of London, but the owners of land reaped the advantages. W,uld it not be more just if rates for public improvements were levied on landowners according to the benefit derived from the carrying out of these improvemeLts, that is to say according to land value?

THE ADVANTAGE TO THE BUSINESS MAN,

Ace] so it is with every public service. Their per- formance makes laud more or less desirable for occupation as they are performed well or badly, as . little or much money is spent in their execution, thus 1 tending to make land value high or low. Surely then,' it is just and business-like that this publicly created' fund should be the source from which public expendi- ture should be taken. Think what this would mean to the ratepayers. Throughout the country there is a Nast amount of valuable land held idle. The value— created by the community—is there, none the less because it is not apparently realised at the moment, yet the owners pay nothing towards public expenses. The taxation of land values would compel these ma ners to contribute. It is imp s,ible to say, until the land of this countiy is valued, how much land is actually idle and not a subject of assessment for rates and taxes, but it is safe to say that the contlibution under land values taxation from this source would enormously relieve those who now have to bear the burden. . Then, under land values taxation a man wruld not contribute more heavily because he was industrious and enterprising ; he would be able to erect a I etter house, fact( ry r r hop, ernpiny better machinery or fittings, without being penalised by heavier rates and taxes. There is another change the taxation rf land values would bring about. Under the influence of the tax idle land would lie brought intosuse and production extended to the advantage of all business men and workers. I shall refer t ) this matter more fully later. ===== 0002601_19120907_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CENTENARY OF THE STEAMSHIP.

A GREAT DAY AT GLASGOW.

Last Saturday was a great day for Glasgow. Steamships stretched far away " doon the river," a battleship that hid its grimness under its bunting was there to make things look official, and showy craft of every kind skimmed the waters. The only sad thing to be recorded is that while all the country side quivered with interest the clerk of the weather showed a strong indifference. A great pageant of shipping was the only thing appropriate to the occasion, for Glas- gow was commemorating the day when the first tiny steamship disturbed the waters of the Clyde and showed that a steam-driven vessel was a practicable thing. These iron castles called battleships, these floating hotels called liners, as well as the whole mis- cellaneous tribe of trading ships have all evolved from that little craft called the " Cornet" which with a four horse-power engine showed a half-sceptical and wonder- ing crowd that a steam boat was a possi- bility. It was an unhappy choice that gave the name " Comet" for comets are things of a day. She was neither comet nor meteor but a fixed and dominating star in the commer- cial firmament. To the steamship as a fixture in the world let the noisy banks of the Clyde gave witness. The event of last Saturday was more than a historical celebration it was an industrial thanksgiving. Of Henry Bell the builder of the first steam- ship the world ought to know a little. He was a stonemason who somehow drifted into the shipbuilding yard and became an engineer. With the tenacity of a true Schotchman who has got a big idea he worked at the task of applying the steam engine for the purpose of driving a vessel. James Watt was supposed to know as much as most men about the possibilities of the steam engine and he dis- couraged him. The Admiralty, which is supposed to know everything, would not listen even when Lord Nelson became his advocate. With fine tenacity and high courage he toiled through many years, and just a century ago made the demonstration on the Clyde that convinced the world of his success. True, she was a vessel of but twenty-five tons, but she sailed from Glasgow and Greenock. Still more sadly true, the stuff of which she was made was got on credit and the boiler upon a promissary note which was never redeemed. But she travelled : that is ' the point that made the day famous. And, if there were creditors who suffered, they suffered for their country's incalculable good. How immense the advantage that has followed upon the fact that the Comet had her home in Glasgow was revealed by a speaker at the banquet (of course there was a banquet) who reminded the assembly that the Clyde built a larger tonnage of ships than the combined shipyards of the United States, Germany and France. Once having got the lead Scotland faith- fully kept it. No wonder that Clydeside had a few kindly things to say about Henry Bell last Saturday. What would Clydeside have been without Henry ? Of course he had the usual pioneer's sorrows and needed a pioneer's skin. As the "Comet" sailed down the Clyde that day, it is credibly reported that the skipper of a sailing vessel bade his crow " kneel down and thank God that ye sail with the Almichty's am n wind, and not with the Deevirs am n fire and brimstone like yon spluttering thing." But this was the mildest of his trials and quite easy to bear. Of course he had no capital. When did an inventor have? Of course someone came along and improved upon his idea. When didn't that happen? Within a year three better boats were sailing the Clyde, and not long after the " Comet " was wrecked. The reader will be prepared for the next item. The inventor died in poverty. We have got used to the story of inventors and it gets monotonous. But it is true. Henry Bell would have died in poverty had not the Clyde Trustees shown their sense of indebted- ness an.l granted him a hundred a year. The Government showed its magnanimity by giving him a lump sum of two hundred. We should like such life-stories to end differently and in an ideal world they would. But it may be we are worrying over it more than the inventor himself. He had done a great thing and perhaps he died content. A.M.

FOOT AND AIOUTII. FRESH LANCASHIRE DISCOVERY CAUSES CONSTERNATION.

Lancashire farmers and butchers were no sooner con- gratulating themselves on the eradication of foot and mouth disease than a fresh outbreak at Chorley was reported. The case has been confirmed, and instead of the removal of restrictions taking place on Monday as had been arranged, a standstill order was issued, affecting a large area round Chorley. Three animals are affected. ti here were thus no cattle,sheep or pigs at Cloosnargh Show, near Preston, on Thursday, and the order puts, an end for the present to the stock sales at the farmers' marl , Preston.

CHESHIRE MILK PRICES RAISED. Cheshire farmers are raising the price of milk for home consumers, and the terms of the next contracts with the town dealers are to be increased. This action is taken because of the bad season. It is stated that many farmers will be compelled to reduce their milking stocks, and there is likely to be a scarcity of milk in many places in the autumn and winter.

CONDEMNED TO DEATH BY CROMWELL'S GOVERNMENT.

The circumstances of his " Disassent from the Government" upon which the Commiss- ioners bestow their official frown will serve to reveal the quality of the man. The Rector of Warrington had begun his career as a Presbyterian, as the appearance of his name in "The harmonious Consent of thel Ministers of the Province" in 1648 clearly shows. But the following three years appear to have witnessed some change in opinion as a result of which in 1651 he was brought into serious difficulty. In that year ministers ' were asked to sign a document known as the "Engagement," an agreement which bound the subscribers to be true to the Common- wealth as then established. The constitution was planned without King or House of Lords and there was much discussion when the paper was sent for signature. Adam Martin- dale of Rostherne tells us that a meeting of Lancashire and Cheshire ministers was held at Warrington, at which some who were "great knockers for disputation" argued almost fiercely against compliance. Robert Yates took the strong course o speaking boldly and in strong terms against the authors of the " Engagement, "in such terms indeed that he was called to answer for them.

This is our first glimpse of the real Robert Yates. In this and in everything he shows himself to have been made of unyielding stuff. That ho had spoken out with unusual vigour is evidenced in the fact that Parlia- ment placed him on trial for his life at Lan- caster. Unfortunately no account of the proceedings has been preserved, but it is known that matters were carried so far against him that he prepared his last speech, and was resolved to face death rather than yield. But the Judge turned matters in his favour and he was acquitted. IMPRISONED DURING THE "FIFTH MONARCHY" PLOTS.

While this was probably his first it was by no means his last acquaintance with prison. Unhappily for us he left no journal, and if he wrote letters describing his experiences they have not survived. Such records, had they been prepared, would have described some years of unusual vicissitude. Oftener than most of his fellows he faces the dreariness of prison in the cause of conscience. Wherever we hear of him in the writings of others he is an unyielding champion and sufferer for the truth.

WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY.

ITS 250th ANNIVERSARY.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
Robert Yates, the Ejected Rector of Warrington.

Robert Yates appears to have been a member of a Blackburn family, and was prob- ably born there in 1612. His name is firstfound in that neighbourhood, and appears upon an Ordinance creating the Presbyterian discipline in Lancashire. Along with Isaac Ambrose, he is set down as a minister of Preston. His appointment to the Rectory of Warrington dates from 1646, and he owed his living to Gilbert Ireland, Esq. In that year he signs the register as "Robert Yates, Rector."

When the rectory was surveyed at the order of the Commonwealth in 1650, it was set forth that he had an income of £2O a year and £l5l is. Bd. in tithes. The description given of "Mr. Yates " is-that he is a man of good life, but he clearly falls under the dis- favour of the Commissioners. The descrip- tion, which is interesting enough to be copied in full, is thus given in the " Commonwealth Church Survey " : Wee doe present that there is a pish Church scituate within Warriugton called Warrington Church, a Mancon howse, barn°, and garden thereunto belonging, one halfe thereof or there- abouts, in the tyssession Jf Mr. Date Yates, ' now minister at Warrington Church, of the yearly value of Thirtie shillings : and the ether halfe or Deere thereabouts is the possession of Mr. : Peetcr Harrison or his assignee, under a Lease formerly made by Sir Thomas Ireland deceased, but whether the same bee expyred or noe we know not, but is of the yearly value of Thit tie shillings: , And that the whole tythes of the said pish of ' Warrington is of the.yearly value or One Hundred fiLie one pounds one shilling and eight pence, and that the said Mr. Yates came in by the quist and pscntation of Gahm Ireland, Esq , who claimes to bee patron and Doner thereof, and alsoe by the ifree ellection of the congregation there ; and that the raid Mr. Yates is a man of good 13 fie, and howbeit Lee dcth disassent from and ot sub- mitt to the rresent g vnemt. And did neglect to observe and keepe the rhtys of humiliation and tbanbesgiving enjoyne,l by the pent piamt ; And that he bath for his salary the yearly sumo of twenty pounds per annum from the said Mr. Ireland.

The journal of Henry Newcome,the ejected minister of Manchester, under date January 10th, 1662, has the following : Mr. Yates and several god people carryed to prison yesterday. A month later comes the entry In ye afternoon Mr. Illingworth and I were cngAged with Mr. Naylor to make friends if wee could ftr Mr. Yates and others unjustly imprisoned at ye present. I could have put off er deavouringe in ye th ; but it is duty and who knows how soono any of us may be in the like conditicn.

In April Robert Yates was released and Mr. Newcome records I had intended to have heard ye commemora- ti sermon but Mr. Yates came in and sate with wee above an houre. Told me ye whole story of his imprisonrcer t. This unhappy episode seems to belong to the "Fifth Monarchy" project and need not detain us here.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
BARTHOLOMEW'S DAY.

August 17th, 1662, the Sunday before Bartholomew's Day was chosen by most of the Non-conforming ministers as the Sunday of farewell. Some of them postponed until the day itself, taking thus the last moment the law allowed to them. At Rostherne the congregation assembled on Bartholomew's Day, summoned as usual by the church bell, to find that 'there was no one to minister. Adam Martindale, who was among them, stepped to the front and enjoyed the luxury of an unexpected Sunday of usefulness and a second valedictory. At Warrington the farewell was heard on August 17 th.and Robert Yates was promptly succeeded by Samuel Ellison. Within a week of Bartholomew the Bishop's emissary appeared with the official instrument of ejectment.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====

order which came from the Bishops Palace of Chester for the suspension of Robert Yates was in the following terms : Whereas in a late Act of Parliament for Uaif irmitie, it is enacted that every Parson, Vicar, Curate, Lecturer, or other Eccle-issticall . . _

pot son neglecting or refusing before the Feast Day of St. Bartholomew, 1662, to declare openly before their respective congregations his assent and consent to the use of all things con- tained in the book° of Common Prayer estab- lished by the said &et shall ipso facto, be deprived; and that every person . . . failing in his subscription . . . shall be utterly dis- abled and, ipso facto, deprived, and his place be void, as if the person so failing were naturally dead. And whereas Robert Yates late Rector of Warrington bath neglected to declare and sub- sot ibe according to the tenour of the said Act, I do therefore declare the church of Warrington to be now void and do strictly charge the said Robert Yates late Rector of the said Church, to forbear preaching, lecturing, or officiating in the _ said c lurch or elsewhere in the diocese of Chester. And the Churchwardens of the said parish of Warrington ate here- by required to secure and preserve the said Parish Church of Warrington from any invasion or intrusion of the said Robert Yates disabled and deprived, as above said, by the said Act. And the Churchwardens are also required, upon sight hereof, to strew this order to the seid Robert Yates and cause the same to be published the next Sunday after in the Parish Church of Warrington before the congregation, as they will I answer to the contrary. Given under my baud this 29th day of August 1662. GEORGE CESTRIEKS. To the Churchwarden of Warrington. There is surely needless cruelty here. To make it the duty of churchwardens to con- vey the intimation to the minister, and to instruct them to preserve the church from his" invasion or intrusion" was a deliberate brutality towards high-spirited and saintly men. But the whole process was one of studied vindictiveness. The sentence "an if naturally dead " fell like a lash upon some tender spirits. With mingled and outraged feelings Oliver Heywood wrote; "Now I am as a dead man out of mind; my voice must no more be heard upon the mountains of Israel. lam not only turned out of the pulpit but out of the Church and must'neither speak nor hear God's word. I am cast out of the Synagogue by men yet the Lord doth not cast me off. "But shall we desist altogether ? The word is as burning fire shut up in our bones and though it cannot blaze in public, as a beacen on a hill, yet should be glowing in private, to enlighten the houses of God's people and warm the hearts of those who are willing to entertain us or our message." There is no period in the life of Robert Yates at which we regret so keenly the

===== 0002601_19120907_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
absence of a journal or letters. Other good men revealed in their writings the storms of feeling that shook their hearts. Doubtless he experienced them all. Perhaps for him as for one of his closest friends the climax of sorrow was "a dumb Sabbath." But the firm spirit which, under Cromwell, had been ready to give up life rather than conviction was not one to be broken by the praspect of poverty and suffering.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONVENTICLE ACT.

Mr. Yates would probably employ himself as most of his fellow sufferers were doing during the days that followed his ejectment. For a year and a half the ministers had little to disturb them except their own inner grief. Most of them set the peaceful example of attending the morning and afternoon services of the Church and listening to the sermons of their successors ; afterwards as a means of exercising their own ministry preaching in their own homes on Sunday evenings. It was against these domestic services that there was directed one of the most cruel Acts that ever disgraced English legislation. Under the title of" Conventicle Act" it was decreed on May 16th 1664, that "if any person above the age of sixteen shall be present at any meeting under colour or pretence of any exercise of religion, in other manner than is allowed by the liturgy or practice of the Church of England, where shall be five more persons than the household, he shall for the first offence suffer three months' imprison- ment, or pay a fine not exceeding five pounds; for the second offence six months' imprison- ment or pay ten pounds; and for the third offence be banished to some of the American plantations for seven years, or pay one hundred pounds, and in case they return, or make their escape, such persons are to be adjudged felons and suffer death without benefit of clergy."

===== 0002601_19120907_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
WHOLESALE PERSECUTION.

England now began to experience an almost universal religious persecution. Ministers would not desist from preaching, nor would their friends cease attendance, and the prisons began to fill with peaceful men and women.

The Quakers refused to regard this inter- ference of the Government and suffered severely in the wretched and disease-laden dungeons of the period. Some of the more thoughtful ministers such as John Howe, bowed their heads to the storm. They saw that they had fallen upon evil days and were content to wait. But upon the majority this new cruelty had another effect. Attendance at the Parish Church had been common to all, but the people began- to separate themselves. It was known that the conforming clergy had pro- moted the Act and in many cases charity gave place to hard and resentful thoughts. The sufferings-of the.ministers endeared them to their friends and ennobled them in the eyes of their enemies. Martyrdom drew to their side a powerful party. We have it on the authority of Dr. Halley that "Mr. Bradshaw, of Hindley, and Mr. Yates, of Warrington, were imprisoned for preaching in private houses," but of the details we know nothing. Those who know the English temperament will not suppose that this wholesale persecu- tion was congenial to the popular mind. After the Act had been in operation three months, Pepys the diarist wrote, "I saw several poor creatures carried by, by constables, for being at a conventicle. They go like lambs without resistance. I would to God they would either conform, or be more wise, and not be catched." There spoke the Englishman; impatient with extremists and with no belief in persecution. But the Nonconformists took little trouble to escape being " catched." A nation that had seen a King's head fall on the scaffold had received an education not soon forgotten.' Tyratny reigned in high places, but quiet courage reigned in the Puritan breast.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FIVE MILE ACT.

Bad as was the "Conventicle Act" it was eclipsed in cruelty by the "Five mile Act." In this achievement Clarendon and Sheldon, who still held high office, did their very worst. Spite more ingenious and unblush- ing never showed itself in legislation. London was plague stricken in 1665. Fresh anger broke out against the Nonconformists because they flocked to the city and preached in the churches from which the clergy had fled,their very heroism being counted as crime Parliament met in Oxford to escape con- tagion and at once proceeded to pass the "Five Mile Act." It was therein decreed that every person in holy orders who had not complied with the Act of Uniformity should take the oath of passive obedience and bind himself not to attempt any altera- tion in the government of either Church or State. Any refusing this oath were for- bidden to act as tutors or schoolmasters. Nor must they come within five miles of a town which sent a member to Parliament, or any place where they had formerly preached.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SCATTERING DAY.

March 24th 1666 was the date upon which the Oxford Act came into operation. On that day Oliver Heywood wrote, as he prepared himself to become a wanderer :

"This is a great scattering day, hundreds of the ministers being by act of parliament banished five miles from the places where they formerly preached. Oh the tears that have been shed for breaking up families, and separating husbands and wives, parents and children, pastors and people."

. . The unblushing malignity a the Five Mile Act is next to incredible. Two thousand of most learned and saintly ministers had been driven from the Church. Fitted by their education to become the tutors of children many were thus eking out a living. By the Oxford Act this one means of liveli- hood was to be taken away. The towns were the spheres of influence and also of friendship; they must therefore be driven into remote villages, there presumably to starve. It was a crueltywhich honest English hearts must view with shame for ever.

As its authors viewed it,it was a triumph; but it was a triumph of malice and one for which no historian is ever likely to offer a defence or an apology. Puritanism was apparently defeated. Pushed from the towns and with the lash of the law over them its ministers were dis- credited and outlawed. Nothing was left but to build up congregations in such remote places as were beyond the scope of the Act, and ancient chapels still linger on lonely hill sides as memorials of men whose zeal remained unquenched even by the ordeal of the wilderness. THE EPISCOPAL RETURNS. In the year of the Five Mile Act and again in 1669 Parliament made enquiries with a view to ascertaining the nature and extent of Dissent throughout the country. The reports are bald, but are valuable as showing the indomitable spirit of the Dis- senters. The Quakers in particular set at defiance every hostile Act of Parliament and the Nonconformists showed a fine disregard for the consequence of the Conventicle Act. Warrington is not referred to in the returns but Winwick, Lymm and Grappenhall get special mention as important lurking places of Dissent.

_ The reports give no details and have few gleams of vinterest to relieve them,sae where

===== 0002601_19120907_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
iCHILDREN'S CORNER, . Conducted by UNCLE CALEB. .N.,...0%,,,N"...",,,...0‘,...1/4"...,"\e-•"\md-mirAd.

A WREN'S NEST IN A BLACKSMITH'S

SHOP.

Every year brings us some new story of the curious resting places of birds. Though usually so shy they are always surprising us by their audacity in choosing a nest in some unexpected or remarkable place. The most interesting instance of a curious resting place I have found this summer is that of a wren which built in a country smithy. The site chosen was in a bolt-rack in a busy country blacksmith's shop. If Father Wren's consent was obtained to this site, it was while the shop was deserted, as he was never seen inside during the building of the nest and incubation, but cheered his more courageous mate with beautiful songs from near by. As the nest progressed most rapidly during the smith's absences, he believed that both birds worked while he was away. The mother bird knew no fear, but flew boldly about, gathering up shavings almost under the smith's hands and feet, approach- ing the nest over a horse that was being shod, and often keeping her place upon it when the smith worked at the vice for welding tires, undaunted by the ringing blows or showers of sparks.

The nest was completed and two eggs were laid on July 7th. Mrs. Wren kept her place during the shoeing of the horses but flew off on close approach. On July 26th, the smith reported five eggs and the little mother sitting close, but, as we peered in at her, she showed signs of nervousness, and finally flew off, revealing five newly hatched fledglings. She came back often enough to:enter with food when only the smith was there, and all the five nestlings grew and throve.

HAPPY JAPAN. • If I'd been born across the seas, In a little home of clean bamboo, Among the flow ring cherry-trees, If I'd been fed on fiAi and rice, The queerest nuts that ever glow, And all the different sorts of teas, If I'd been need to jinrikisha, And never seen a railroad car,— Perhaps it wouldn't seem so nice To be a Japanese. -CLARA E. DOLLIVER, THIS WEEK'S STORY. DIFFICULTIES IN THE WAY.

A teacher tells a good story against herself. She had been very strict in requiring written excuses from the mothers in case of absence. The morning of the big snowstorm only a few of the children made their appearance. The next day they all came with written excuses except one tot, named Willie. When asked for his, he said, "I did ferdit it."

He Was cautioned to bring it the next day. Willie's mother was quite disgusted. It seemed to her that any one ought to know the reason for his absence. The next morning he arrived all rosy with the cold, and handed the teacher his excuse. It read :

"Dear Miss C— : Little Willie's legs are fourteen inches long. The snow was two feet deep. Very truly yours, Mrs

ME MARTINGALE.

BY DAVID CHRISTIE MURRAY, Author of "A Life's Atonement," " Doh Mar- tin's Little Girl," "A Rogue's Cosscience," Mount Despair," "A Race for Millions," &e.

John Henry Mackenzie was one of those fortu- nate people who are said to be born with a silver spoon in the mouth. Mackentsie's spoon was not so large as to be an inconvenience to him, nor so small that it failed to nupply him with anything he wanted—that is to say, any- thing in reason for a young man wtose father had been a tradesman in a country town, and the habits of whose early life had been simple even to severity. He had an incosne of about £650 per annum, and when he put his hat on he covered all his responsibilities Until he came of age he was articled to a 'elicitor, and Ire practised under his employer foss three years. Then his father died, and the young man came into his kingdom. He resolved to go up to London and to read for the Bar. Ile entered himself at Gray's Inn, and for a month he thought often of the old dad in Barfield Ceme- tery, and worked assiduously. In the course of his second month in London he thought less of the old dad and the old home, and his studies were less eagerly pursued. The two things seemed to go together somehow, and when his thoughts ranged back to old days he always felt impelled to stick to his work and to justify the simple pride his father had shown at the fact that he had lifted up a son to be a member of the professional classes. And so, as memory faded and grew less ur- gent, John Henry Mackenzie grew less inclined to industry, and more inclined to take life easy with a band of young gentlemen who were en- gaged, or supposed to be engaged, in studies similar to his own. This contingent drank more than was good for it, and smoked more than was good for it, and sat up o' nights, and played , poker, and attended suburban meetings, and backed horses more than was good for it. And, although it was likely that some of his com- panions would one day, in the course of nature, be a great deal better off than he was, yet Mac- kenzie had a larger current income than any of them, and was master of his own resources. Outside that solid little income upon which he might rely for life—the principal of which was tucked away in Consols—he had a few loose hundreds at the bank—his late father's floating balance—and thus he was a sort of small Triton amongst the minnows with whom he swam. ITe could lend money to young men who were social swells in comparison with himself, who had been at Eton or Harrow and one or other of the 'Varsities; and because he was country btrd, and had been educated lat a day-school, he felt uplifted by his present companionships, and thought he was seeing life in fine society. But in a while it became evident to John Henry that some of his friends were much hot- ter at promises than at payment, and that they were vastly more practised and expert at bor- rowing than at lending. The floating balance went, and his own quarter's income went. whilst ' the next pay day was eight weeks off; and John Henry became actually hard-up, and had to borrow at interest. Since he hind dropped a good share of the floating balance at poker, it was evi- dent that somebody had Avon in the games in which, he had been engaged. And a game at which one man' won was so evidently a game nt which another man might win that young Mackenzie went more ardently for poker than ever, and got deeper into the mire. Not very deep as yet—not over shoe-tops even—but deep enough to make his financial footsteps drag a good deal. As the simplest and readiest way out of his troubles he sold out £l,OOO worth of Stocks in the Consolidated Funds, and so set the floating balance going again. But nobody ever had such rotten luck as John Henry. It is a remarkable thing that nobody ever did have such rotten luck at poker as anybody else who makes a habit of losing at it. I have hardly ever known a poker player who hasn't had the rottenest luck that any man ever had in the his- tory of the game. That is the one great peculiarity of games of mingled chance and skill. You take five players, and out of four of them each man will have had rottener luck than any one of the other three. lam not a mathe- matician, and I cannot offer a scientific explana- tion of the fact; but I am an observer, and I can vouch for its existence. Bridge had not been revealed to man in the days of which I write, and poker held its vogue, and was just as fascinating and just as useful to its votaries. It was John Henry's fortune to hold three aces against four twos or to meet a full-house of kings and fives against queens and nines. In these circumstances, what could the poor man do but play to the limit, and what could happen but disaster? At the end of his second year the youthful Mackenzie's annual income was less by forty-five pounds sterling than it had been, indicating in round figures a loss of fifteen hundred pound. He began to take fright a little. He began to review the dreams with which he had entered upon his studies for the Bar, and to believe he was the most stupendous aS 3 the world had seen. One night, when the floating balance had drifted on the rocks and had suffered shipwreck, and his noisy comrades had gone home, lie sat down and thought about things, and began to take audit of his wasted money and his wasted days. Something of health and youth had been wasted along with the money and the hours. Youth has an extraordinary faculty for feeling old, and Mackenzie, under the cross-examination of his own spirit, was more aged than Methusaleh. He and his set habitually drank too much whisky, and took too little exercise; and these are not practices which, when long continued, tend to cheerfulness of thought in solitude. Mackenzie had a bad two or three hours with himself in the witness-box and his conscience as counsel for the prosecution. The prosecuting ,Mackenzie cut the defendant Mackenzie's case to ribbons. Then, turning judge, Mackenzie summed up dead against Mackenzie. The jury--still Mackenzie— found him guilty, with no extenuating circum- stances, and the sentence passed upon him was that he should despise himself as long as he should live. Who has not known these bitter hours of self. arraignment and self-condemnation? If there be any such, his case is parlous. Mackenzie packed in the grey of the morning beforp he went to bed, and he chose the largest of his portmanteaux to make room for the law books he had so long neglected. Then, with his mind all seething with resolve, he undressed and tumbled into bed, and passed the first wholly sleepless night he could remember. In the early hours of the day he transacted a little business with his banker, and fixing to his door the legend "Out of Town," he took cab to the Hol- born terminus, a.nd booked at hazard for a place _ he had never so much as heard of until that moment. An hour later he was the sole passen- ger deposited at a country railway-station. It was summer weather, and the bright sun- shine lay broadcast down to sleep about the drowsy fields. There was but a single human creature in sight—a railway porter, who was catch- ing flies on a sun-baked wail at the far end of the platform. Him Mackenzie ineffectually hailed, and at last, drawing close, found preternaturally hard of hearing; but, after the expenditure of much sound and patience, learnt that there was a hostel called the Anglers' Rest a mile and a- half, or, it might be, two, miles away, where • genlemen sometimes stayed. The folks ud send up a barrow for his honour's luggage. So, having made himself sure of his route, and having tipped the porter, Mackenzie marched leisurely towards the Anglers' Rest, a neat, quiet little place in the centre of a neat, quiet little vil- lage. He could have ham and eggs for luncheon, so it seemed, with tea or a beer of the house's own brewing, but nothing more than that till even- ing, wben the butcher's cart made its hi-weekly round. He ordered the ham and eggs and the beer of the house's own brewing, having tasted which he decided on tea for the future, and held to that decision until he tasted the tea at his next meal. But the ham was home-fed and cured, and was grilled to a turn, and the eggs were fresh, and there was a dusky loaf that smelt of home, and English butter fresh from a neigh- bouring farm. Mackenzie, after a satisfying luncheon, filled and lit his pipe, and sat down in a broad old armchair, staring drowsily at the smoke wreaths and the sunlight and greenery beyond them. What with a two miles' walk in the midday heat, a square meal, the soothing influence of his pipe, and the sleepless night he had spent, he trailed off into a doze, and in that doze he heard S voice of great sweetness and beauty. It was a lady's voice. Ho had no notion of what it said, but at a silvery burst of girlish laughter became broad awake, and, peeping through the fuchsias and geraniums which were ranged along the sill of the open window, he saw a girl with a rein thrown over her arm, and a pony standing nuzzling at her hand. She wore a riding habit of dark blue, and a natty little bowler hat, and the figure seemed to young Mackenzie to match the voice. "Give me a glass of water, Mrs. Martin," she said, and the old landlady's footsteps shuffling over the brick-paved entrance to the house be- spoke the fulfilment of the request. Whilst Mackenzie stood looking out of the Window the girl ttlfrned, and he saw her face. Some thought in her mind at that moment caused her to smile, and Mackenzie thought that she was the very prettiest and most charming

,

Tho vision drank her glass of water when it came, lingered two or three minutes to gossip with the landlady, skipped into the saddle as lightly as a bird drops on the hough, and then away she went, taking the sunlight with her. Mackenzie, talking that evening with the land- lady, made many roundabout approaches to the theme he most wished to talk about, and when at length he dared to mention the fact. that he had seen a young lady at the door, Mrs. Martin opened a sort of conversational flood-gate on him.
