Mackenzie stayed on at the Anglers' Rest, and read law diligently, and swam and fished and boated. Anglers came down from time to time and stayed a day Or two, but Mackenzie was a fixture. Twice a week, perhaps, on an average, he had the joy of taking off his hat to Miss Red- fern. This great thing happened on a Sunday, when ho was more devoutly regular in his at- tendance than be had even been before.

Some of the people in the neighbourhood got to know him, and with such as were on terms of in- timacy with the Vicar's household he was gracious; and, being a well-looking, well-groomed, and well-mannered youngster, he found his patience at last rewarded. Having been ' once intro- duced to the Vicar he took so marked an interest in whatever that excellent gentleman was in- terested in that he quickly became a persona grata, and was almost like an inmate of the house—he went in and out with so much free. dom and was made so easily welcome. Now, the Vicar was a man with a craze. He was a mathematician, and lie hail devised a mar- tingale by means of which it was possible f7r any person who adhered to it rigorously te a given sum according to the amount of invested. He had reams of calculations about it, and he could prove by demonstration to any- body who cared to listen to him that if only his principles and his cloth would allow him .to, do it he could go over to Monte Carlo with a mete hundred pounds to begin with and come back a millionaire. All he would have to do would be to double his capital and then to . double his stakes, and to go on repeating that simple pro- cess until at last the business syndicate who run the bank would wind lip the concern. "M. Blanc," he would say, "has set the world a puzzle. It is a very ingenious puzzle, and amongst the thousands who have beaten their brains against it there are scores who have an, rived at an apparent solution of it which has led them on to ruin. But there is a key to the problem, and I have found it." Then he would bring out two or three num- bers of "Les Permanences." a publication which at that time was guarded like a guilty secret. It gives all the numbers which have turned up at a given table for a week; and on these old num- bers he had based his system, which certainly looked not less than perfect to Mackenzie. He was a born gambler, and the idea excited him. Ile bought a miniature wheel, and a roulette cloth in proportion with it. and & bag of counters quite disproportionate to them both, to rerresent the fabulous wealth of the syndicate. The Vicar and be spent many hours under loca and key in the study, and the reverend gentleman played against John Henry, and ex- pounded his system, winning bone counterr the teacupful every evening. He made a COT'. vert of the young gentleman from the first, but he was quite unfeignedly horrified to find that his pupil refused to take a purely platonic view of the discovery, and proposed to put it into action at the tables.

"I'm not spending a third of my income clown here, sir," said John Henry "You can't in a little place like this. I'll just take over what- I've saved, and I'll have a flutter."

"I don't like the idea," said the Rev. Mr. Redfern. "I am sorry I ever led you into it. To rue it has had the interest' of a mathemati- cal problem merely. I really wish, John,, that I could perauade you to have nothing to do with it."

"It's too late for that, sir, ,I'm afraid. Pm pledged to start to-morrow with. a friend of mine."

z am sorry," said the good man. "I re- proach myself." But Mackenzie took the martaingale to Monte Carlo, and with it a sum of £2OO sterling. And day by day he and his chum sat at the table, and the chum pricked off the game for him, and the pile dwindled and dwindled and dwindled until all was gone, and they came back sadder than they went, and a little--a very little--wiser. "The system doesn't act, sir," he reported, mournfully. "De Haviland and I each dropped two hundred on it."

"The system does act," declared the mathe- matician. "I know exactly what has happened. You have tried to better it. You have hedged against it, and have lost faith in it becauso •-** some paltry momentary losses. Didn't we test fully hero fur weeks? Don't the pertnanences confirm the principle? Have you ever detected a flaw?"

"No, sir," tito young man had to acknow- ledge. "It looked all right. But I give you my word that we never left it once. Do Haviland has the figures, sir."

" Nonsense!" said the cleric: "Nonsense?"

And because the Vicar was his elder, and be- cause he was a clergyman, and partly because he was the father of the girl with whom Mac- kenzie was in love, he took the rebuke in silence, undeserved as it was. But ho got his chum to send down the figures, and he at down with his bag of counters and read out the winning num- bers. And the Rev. "Well, I don't know how we went wrong," said Mackenzie, ruefully. "We followed the martingale all through.' "No, no," cried the Vicar. "Impossible! The facts demonstrate it. You lost, and the system wins. Wins on your own figures." _ _

"I wish you'd come over to Monte, and have a try, sir." The parson shook his head. "Oh, I know how you feel about it, sir. But don't you i think that n a kind of way it's a duty, Mr. Red- fern?"

'A duty?" cried the Vicar, gasping. " duty?" "Yes, sir," urged John Henry: "There's the church without a spire. You might have that reredos you've set your heart upon. And a new organ. And as for coals and blankets for the winter club —" He broke off there, and said no more, but they played on John Henry's figures more than once, and the Vicar always broke the bank. THE AEROPLANE AS A MEANS OF PUBLIC

TRANSPORT.

Referring to this subject, " Engineering " remarks —Things may run smoothly at first. The first serious accident will reduce a bad effect on the public mind, but it will pass away. Then will come another periad of smooth running, with, prrbaps, a few trivial mishaps. Companies will continue to spring up all over the world to exploit the mammoth long-dhtanco machine capable of carrying from 50 to 100 passengers. The second serious accident will occur, but its effect on the public mind will WOO pats away, as will that of tbe three or four others which will follow in due course. As machines increase in number the periods between serious accidents will steadily shorten until they are of almost daily occurrence. Gradually the results of the treacherous gust or the forced descent on rough ground —the long &laps occasiooed,b3 waiting for favourable weather at different stations—will sink into the public mind, and faitti in the passive-outface machine as a passenger-carrying vehicle will die out. The world will become convinced that the aeroplane is essentially a capricious flyer—good enough, es someone has put it, "to flirt a ith,' but nothing mere.

EX-LIEUT. MONTAGU ACQUITTED. -- Tragedy at Constantinople Accidental.

The trial of ex-Lieutenant Herbert G. Montagu at Constantinople for the alleged murder of an English- man named Dayan, a traveller for a Sheffield ailver firm, ended on Tuesday in his acquittal. Mr. Montagu (says Reuter) was defended by Sir Edwin Pears, who was assisted by Dr. Abdul Mnjid, a prominent London Anglo-Indian barrister, and the prosecution for the Crown was conducted by Mr. Cuchet and a local English counsel. Mr. Montagu is only 24 years of age. He was a lieutenant on probation in the 9th Battalion Royal Fusiliers (City of London Regiment) until the end of September 1911. When the war broke out between Turkey and Italy he jotned the Arab forces outside Tripoli and led them in many of their dating charges against the Italians. Attacked with dysentery, be returned for a few weeks to ngland, sod was " shadowed" the whole way by Italians. His commis- sion was cancelled owing to his having broken the regulation which forbids Army officers giving informa- tion to the Plias, and he returned to Tripoli. The death of Dayan occurred on the night of August oth at an hotel in Constantinople. Montagu has always stated that the affair was the result of an accident.

WINNIPEG AS A GRAIN CENTRE

now stands pre-eminent on the North American Continent. A statement of the facts was presented by the Winnipeg Grain Exchange to the Duke of Connaught on his recent visit to the city. "The first export shipment of grain from the Canadian West was less than 200 bushels and was sent out in sacks. To-day grain is shipped by the cash ad in traius running as high as 50 to 00 cars each. We have, at the bead of the Canadian lakes, 30,000,0(0 bushels storage capacity; the Canadian fleet of lake freighters has a capacity of 9,000,000 bushels; and our ports are thronged, during the open water, with the enormous lake freighters from the American ports. The first shipment went out in bags on a river steamer to St. PAUL To-dry freighters carrying 360,C00 bushels sail from our lake ports to eastern terminals. Winnipeg as a handler of grain Las surpassed the famous markets of Chicago and Minneapolis, in 1911, 101,320,250 bushels of wheat being handled at Winnipeg, BB against 93,617,830 bushels at Minneapolis, and 42,629,751 bushels at Chicago."

FLOOD DAMAGE IN NORFOLK.

It is impossible yet to estimate the financial loss to Norwich caused by the recent floods. Most of the sufferers have now returned to their homes from the shelters provided for them. Many of the houses are completely

ruined, as shown in our picture. In Norfolk there are 52 bridges damaged or destroyed, and the district surveyor estimates that to repair them and the roads £27,000 will be required.

THE POWER OF WAVES

Some of the forces of Nature, sa3 a " Engineering," osn be meaaured, such, for example, as the velocity of the wind, the pressure of eater due to a given bead, and the rate of erosion on a coast line ; but. Smeaton was correct when, in referring to certain other powers of Nature, he said : "They are subject to no calculr.- tiop." Thus it is that the marice engineer's task is often a difficult ens. The force with which a wave stikes a plane surface, while it may be gauged approxi- mately, cannot he accurately measured ; for while a marine dynamometer will record a certain blow, this really does not represent the force of impact of the wave against, say, the upright free of the sea-wall to which the instrument is fixed, for the wall is non- elastic, while the dynamometer is usually fitted with springs or plungers, end is therefore an elactio body. The force of impact of a wave will depend ripin five things :-1. The nature of the wave (ehether a tidal or a wind wavel. 2. Its length sod height. 3. Its velocity. 4. The nature of the body against which the wave strikes—whether elastic or rigid. 5. The shape and position ef such a body—whether it has a vertical or sloping face. The grea'est recorded force of impact of a wave was obtained at Dunbar, in the county of East Lothian, when a force of 31 tons per eq. ft.on the surface was registered. A force of tbree tors per Eq. ft. has been registered frequently. There can be no doubt that higher results than these may be obtained. The displacement of heavy. bodies, and the height to which a wave rises after strikic g a vertical face, would indicate that the record given by the dynamometer is less than the actual force exertel The displacement of heavy bodies by a wave is well illustrated during every severe storm by the washing away of a portion of a sea-wall and promenade on some part of our coast, or by other damage done. During the construction of the Parade Extersion Works at Bridlington, for example, a number of blocks of stone, each weighing from three to four tons, were washed out of position and carried several yards away. This occurred during a severe north-westerly gale. Dining a storm at Wick huge blocks of concrete, weighing 1350 and 2500 tons respectively, were dis- placed. This seems scarcely credible, and doubt has been raised as to whether this movement was due entirely to wave action ; it would seem to be within the bounds of possibility, however, when we consider that in 1894, during a storm, P. large portion of the breakwater at the Bilbao harbour, estimated to weigh 1700 tons, was overturned, doe enirely to wave action. The old Beaconsfield sea- wall at Bridlington was entirely demolished by a storm, or series of storms, which occurred about eighteen years ago, and huge pieces of the wall of concrete and brick, weighing 20 tons or more, are now to be seen 'upon the forshorP. Dui ing the storm which occurred ' in November, 1911, Much damage was done by the sea at Heysham, large pieces of the sea-wall being washed away. Other examples might be given, but these will , doubtless suffice to show that the force indicated by dynamometers is really less than the force actually exerted. It should also be noted that when is wave strikes a body, its action is continuous as long as the wave lasts, and not momentary, as when a solid strikes a solid. It is this continuity of impact that causes the damage. Mr Then as Stevenson put it in a simple way when he likens it to " a continuous succession of cannon balls felling on the body."

WOMEN'S GHAT.

The Professions! Shopper.—There are commission agents of all sorts and conditions in the world of com- merce, and to the list must be added that of the pro- fessional shopper, who buys for clients abroad or in the country, and obtains a five per cent discount for herself from the Landon tradesmen. Shopping is a business which requires ability and tact, and were it not so much a matter for individual taste, there would be ample scope for the services of a professional buyer. The rrt of shopping varies in different counties, and the Anglo Indian, who returns after a long absence in the East, is apt to make rcistakes. A lady, accustomed to the leisurely bargaining, dear to the Oriental trader, recently entered a West End shop in London, to buy some glover," How much a pair?" she asked the shop- woman. "Four and sixpence" was the reply, "I will give you two and sixpence," suggested the customer, who, seeing the astonished look of the shop girl, only then realised that she was not east of Suez. The pro- fessional shopper would he risenl to m )st Anglo- Indi,ns, who are much given to sending home shopping commissions to ft iends or relatives, to whom this busi- ness means some amount of trouble, anxiety and responsibility. They would he prepared to recommend the professional shopper, who once she established her reputation bir being depended upon, should have no difficulty in doing well in this new avocation. Be-Jewel led r:hoes and Stockings.—Paris seems determined to vie with New Yolk in encouraging wealthy women to outddo one another in the splendour of their dresses and jewels. The Americus millioniiress Las not yet taken to drinking dissolved pearls, as Cleopatra did, but she has been seen out with diamonds in her she e heels, and now the flue de Is Paix has decided that the wealthy Frenchwoman must wear stockings spun out of fine gold and silver thread. They cost from .23 or £l, to £lO a pair ahnost too inexpen- sive for the tqste of those given to parading their wealth. People can get a lot of discomfort out of riches, and there would be.some inconvenience in having footwear and hosiery that had to be kept in a safe. The owner could not leave her shoes outside her door, in a hotel, to be cleaned in the morning, and she would have to send her hosiery not to the bundry, but to the jeweller to be cleaned. Fur-trimmed Garments.—Certain furs, such as sable, and chinchilla, are always worn but the cheaper sorts come and go with each season. Ermine is decidedly an expensive fur and women who have good sets of it wear them whether the pelt is "in'' or " cut." This sesson it is to have a vogue, in common with ruinniver, which is ermine with just the tips of the tails inserted at more or less irregular iutervls, instead of the whole of these append..ges as in ermine. Already dresses for outdoor weer have been trimmed with these pretty furs and a little tippet of either is a modish accessory to serge and other autumn dresses. A very chic hat of white felt luta its brim lightly bound with black velvet, its sole garniture a strip of ermine, scarcely two inches in width. This is laid negligently round the base of the crown, the ends resting on the brim. One of the latter is finished with a small bead, the other has 'no ornament. Afternoon and evening dresses will he extensively trimmed with the very rarrowest borderings of furs. There will be very little restriction as to the pelt, but all edging must be as narrow as possible. Quite wide single hands are used at the bottom of outdoor dresses, a fashion not to be commended.

A Pretty Home Frock.—Narrow braids will be exten- sively used on tailored suits, and much ingenuity is shown in the u e of this alsvaya popular trimming. Dress was never more easily adapted to practical pur- poses, than it is at present, and the favour still extended to the sensible, three-piece dress, is only one instance among many, of the truth of this statement. Another practical fashion is the little frock of some pretty pastel colour, not too light or too dark, made of cashmere or very lice cloth, which adapts itself equally for afternoc n or evening wear. his is easily built at home, and is at its best when:simply trimmed with two or three narrow pleats on the skirt, whilst the bodice is relieved with a yoke of net or lace. Tbe Magyar style of sleeve is iuited to this gown, although it is to be relegated, in future, to blouses and "rest " gowns only. The Serviceable Overall.—Nowadays, every woman who paints, or interests herself in gardening, cooking, or any other outside, or household pursuit, provides herself with dainty and becoming overalls, which, if chosen with care make a plain woman look quite pretty and a pretty one still prettier. Where tho washing bill has to be considered, a plain material, not too light iu colour, should be selected, and for this purpose no material at present on the market,is quite so serviceable and satisfactory as casement cloth, to which every woman owes a debt of gratitude, since it adapts itself most kindly to all kinds of Lass. A girl who has no qualms as to the amount of her weekly washing bill, may choose spotted cambric, linen, zephyr, holland, or galatea, whilst thisseason's pretty soft ginghams in the most charming tints are admirably suited for the making of overalls. Two or three pockets are necessary i if this garment is to he a success ; t should cover the bodice entirely, and either have long sleeves, or be provided with sleevelets that protect the wrist, and lower part of the arm. New Millinery.—Dats for the autumn are neat in design, their ,individuality depending on their shape and the fsbrics from which they are made. For wear- ing with tailor-mades, there are pedal and chip straws, and hats of felt and plush. The postilion shape, trimmed with cocks' feathers, is making a bold bid for favour, The Marquis is another favourite, and this

also is trimmed with an abuuo of plumaga. Green, in a splendid range of tones, is greatly to the fore. Small hats, of the jockey typo, are also shown. They are seamed in the crown, fit tightly to the head, and have a stitched brim. The " Niniche," so popular a few seasons ago, has been revived. The crown is very full and heavily draped, the brim flit with an upward tendency behind. White felt hats, of diverse but small shapes, are undoubtsdly first favourites just now. The Dancing Season.—The "new dancing" which many people think less giaceful than the old, will be seen in every ball room during the winter and autumn season. The old waltz has almost entirely disappeared like the period dances—such as the quadrille, and the lancers—and its place is now taken by the Boston waltz with its side glide, and the one stop and the two step, which are the popular dances of the moment. Seeing that public whist drives have teen declared illegal, sub- scription dances seem likely to be more popular than ever this season with the youth of middle class society. We have not, in this country any State-aided scheme for training children in dinning, as there is in Russia, but a great that more attention than was formerly the case is being given to calisthenics in the schools, with the result that dancing, which is conducive to a good carriage and graceful walk, is becoming more and more popular with all classes. Mint lea—Old-time country people lal grrat faith in the virtues of herbs, and the labiate plants, hut we have never heard of their drinking mint tea, at their daily meals. This, it seems, is a fa.rly popular beverage with fashionable women In Russia, and one of the Grand Duchesses haa introduced it among Society people in London,as an alternative to tea and ooffee. It is made from specially prepared dried mint, in the same way as ordinary tea, and is not of unpbasant-flavout. But it has none of the restorutive properties of ordin- ary tea, and spparettly the only reason for taking it Is that it is good for the complexion and free from the harmful effrc's, %shirt) the doctors tell us sometimes result from indulgence in tea or coffee.

" COMES IN CHEAPER, TOO.

Some time ago a correspondent wiote : My doctor one day proscribed a nasal wash for me. The prescrip- tion was an abbreviation of two Lain words, and it was unintelligible to the layman. I took it t ) a druggist, and be filled a little bottle from a big bottle on his shelf, and ho charged me half-a-crown. The little bottle held about one-fourth as much as the I big bottle. I asked him if I might see the big bottle. It bore a label which told its contents and the name of the chemists that prepared it. " What is the price of this big battle? " I asked. " Four shillings." I bought the big bottle for four shilli..gs instead of havmg the little bottle filled from it four times at a cast of ten shillings. The next time I saw the doctor I told him what I had done. "You were very right," he said. "Did I prescribe the thing in this way? It was the force of habit." " Don't you think that an intelligent layman might be trusted with some knowledge of what goes up hie nose or down his throat? " " Tho whole system," he replied, " is foolish and out t f date— of cow 80, of course." I now ask my doctor to translate his pre- scriptions, to explain what the stuff is that I must use or swallow, and precisely what effact he expects it to have. I am perfectly walling to take even bread pills, but I prefer to take them in English. One could easily multiply such things horn one's own experience but, for the present, nuf said.' OTHER TROUBLES. After all, as was shown in:the " Children's column " last week, there is such a thing as " Troublesome English." As for the French which our youngsters are supposed to learn nowadays—dear, dear ! Much of it seems to me to amount to very little more than Slithers acquired oa a pop visit to the Continent. "Did you learn any French while you were in Paris?' asked Bildad, meeting Slithers:shortly after his return " Oh,'a little," said Slithers. " Not so very much though. I got so I could say cigarette in French." " Good ! " said Bildad. " What is cigarette in French? " " Cigarette," said Slithers. Bye-the- bye I somewhere read a while ago that with a view to assisting tourists and visitors to London in finding their way, securing the right vehicle, &c., steps have been taken to coach some of the City police in easy conversational French. A course of grammar being quite out of place for such a purpose the policemen appear to have been taken in hand pretty much as I myself was hag ago by a certain Mr. Thomas —that is, according to a simplified system which bears the reputation of being the one for imparting practical French with a minimum expenditure of time and labour, the desired result being attained by absolutely ignoring unimportant detail, and drilling thoroughly in what is really essential. By constant strict application to this principle, the constables have (we are told) in an astonishingly short space of time, been made able to answer exactly the questions most likely to be asked by foreigners desiring guidance in this great city. One can anticipate what our Warrington Esperantists will say to all this, and I do not forget that, when their Congress was held at Cambridge five years ago, a local police sergeant was through that medium ableto converse with and materially assist a number of foreign visitors who happened to be stranded in that city late at night. MAY I TELL ONE • The delicious example from Mrs. Helen ' Mar's may I tell you a story?' quoted in last week's notes reminded ma of a precisely similar experience of my own. It will be remembered that Miss Mar's titbit was to the effect that a rich American who had bought a Venus de Milo recovered damages from an Express Company because the statue arrivrd with mutilated arms, and that, on the top of all this, one in whose hearing Miss Mar repeated the story was unable to understand why "if the company had smashed the goods" they should not be liable! I buttonholed a man in the street, and compelled his attention while I told him Mr. Hooley's story. " Yis," said Mr. Hooley, "there was a toime win oi wuz just ' Hooley,' or ' Mick.' Thin it got to Misther Hooley,' an' afther that to ' Mikeyul Hooley, Esquoire ' ; an' agin to 'Alderman Mikeyul Hooley, Esquoire, Jay Pay ' ; an' lasht Sunday mornin' win Oi wint to church, they all , rose up az Oi entered, an' sang out,' Hooley, Hooley, 'holey, Lord God Almighty! '" The simple chap into whose wondering ears I poured this silences heard me courteously to the end, and, just at the point wheie the laugh was supposed to come in, he ' said plaintively and with a puzzled expression over- spreading his fat countenance, " Well, but, you know, there is a hymn that goes like that ! Before doing this the Medical Officer was required to give to the person suffering or to some person being in charge of the patient three clear days' notice of his intention to make such application and notice of the time and place where it would be made. There was also a clause as to making good any financial loss to those dependent on the sufferer, but in that case, said Mr. Turton, the person suffering was in receipt of Poor Law relief, so that there would be ro loss inflicted on the family. The man lodged in one of the streets in Greenbank, with his wife and two children, where four other adults also resided, so that there were six adults and two children living in a small house of two living- rooms and two bedrooms. The man stated that he slept in the front living-room on a sofa, and that room was occupied by the other people in the daytime. The Medical Officer and Poor Law authorities had tried to induce the man to go to hospital,but he was very stupid in the matter. Dr. Buchan gave evidence as to the very infectious state the man was in, and said that proper precautions to prevent the spread of infection could not be taken. He bad personally notified the man, and tried to per- suade him to go to hospital, but in vain. The Bench made an order for the patient to be detained in hospital for the full three months.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this authority was held at the Earlestown Town Hall on Monday when there were present : Messrs CB b' Borron (in the chair), E T Evans, T Ritchie' W Davies, NV Roughley, W Goalie, C Cole (clerk), R T Surtees (engineer). It was resolved that the following reports of the Sub-committee of the Itb, 23rd, and 30th July last be approved and adopted viz. :—(a) It was resolved that as regards the admis- sion of free students : (1) That boys and girls be admitted to the Technical School for one session with- out payment of fee, provided that : (a) They left the day school after November last, 1011; (b) They have been in regular attendance at a public elementary day school for at least two years prior to the date of leav- ing ' • (c) They enter for a full course of study ; (d) They attend regularly and do the necessary homework ; (e) A parent or guardian signs a form whereby the fee which has been remitted may be recovered if the attendance or work is unsatisfactory. (2) In the case of fee-paying students attending the Technical School for the first time, free admission be given for the following, but not for any subsequent year, provided that attendance, conduct, and progress Lave all been satisfactory throughout the sessiOn. The student must make 85 per cent, of the possible number of attendances and attend the examinations.—The Secretary reported that Harold Dring, a student at the school, had secured the first prize for mechanical engineering (machine drawing and:designing) Grade 11., offered by the Goldsmiths' Company, in connection with the City and Guilds of London institute, and also the Institute's bronze medal, the value of the prize being £2 102. He also reported that in connection with the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes, another student, Lloyd Payne, had obtained a second prize for arithmetic (junior), and also a grouped course certificate.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIDNES CARTER'S FAILURE. A VICTIM OF MOTOR HAULAGE AND INDUSTRIAL STRIKES. At the Liverpool Bankruptcy Court on Monday,l before Mr Registrar Cooper, Henry Hayes Edgerley, an elderly raan, of 31, Sutton's-lane, Widnes, appeared for public examination, the statement of affairs which had been filed, showing liabilities of £132 123 9cl, and assets of £l2O 8s 2d, the deficiency being only £3 Is 7d. The debtor has filed his own petition. The largest ' creditor, a Mr Richardson, has obtained judgment against the debtor for £3ll and costs ; but the debtor claims a set-off to the amount of £322 for Norse hire. The Official Receiver appeared to conduct the exami- nation. Mr T J Peters oppeared for Mr Richardson,' and Mr Poole appeared for the debtor. In examination by the Official Receiver, the debtor said be had been in business twenty-nine years, having started with a capital of £l5O, which came to him on the death of his father. The cause of his trade going down was motor haulage and strikes. He had been employed largely by a brewery to deliver their beer, for which he was paid per barrel, and of late they had adopted motor baulags, and be had in consequence lost the trade ' • also he bad lost horses by death, three having died in a twelvemonth, and the carters' strike and other strikes had caused him loss. The examination was closed.

WIDNES SUPERINTENDENT TO RETIRE. Superintendent Robert Strickland, of the Widnes Division of the Lancashire County Constabulary, is shout to retire, and Widnes will thus lose a popular official. The superintendent has been in the service of the county for forty-one years, and in recognition of this will be presented with the King's Coronation medal at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions next Thursday. Joining the Constabulary in 1871, he was drafted to the chemical borough,wlaere he set up a life-long connection with the town. Widnes in those days was a rural resort, with only two or three constables to maintain law and orde-. He succeeded the late Superintendent Joseph Keighley eight years ago Stationed as he was in Widnes during the great chemical boom thirty years ago, when labour-was scarce, and men of the labouring class earned £5 and £6 per week, he saw much strenuous work in the rougher quarters of the town. On one occasion, while endeavouring to arrest a burglar, the man stabbed him in the side, but the officer secured his man. Fourteen pounds worm of goods were found in his possession, and inquiries revealed that he had escaped from Chester Castle. During a street row the superintendent was pushed through a plate-glass window by a turbulent crowd, and was laid up for some weeks as a result.

DEATH OF MR. W. R. CLARKE._ WIDNES.

The —death o-OCtu:;:eci suddenly on Tuesday morning of Mr W it Clarke, at the are of fifty-four years, at his residence, Deacon-road, Widnes. The deceased gentle- man was at work as manager of the Widnes Alkali Works, belonging to the United Alkali Company, es recently as Wednesday last. He caught a chill, which subsequently developed into pneumonia. His condition weakened considerably on Monday night, and he passed away as stated. "W. R." as he was familiarly known, was one of the most popular public men in the town, and his demise will occasion a serious loss. In 1903 he aspired to Council honours, and was elected a representative of Sirnm's Cross Ward, for which he was twice returned unopposed. Among his numerous Dublin duties he was deputy-chairman of the Health Committee, chairman of the Hospital Sub- Committee, and one of the pioneers of the motor-bus service in the town. His knowledge of educational matters was valuable, and he took a deep interest in technical education. For many years he was promi- nently associated with the Widnes Sports, Widnes F.C., Widnes C.O and was the town's representative on the IN,C.A.A A.

RUNCORN URBAN COUNCIL. ROAD IMPROVEMENT FOR MOTOR TRAFFIC. Runcorn Urban Council on Tuesday evening discussed the Highway Committee's proposal to widen Mough- land-lane, tire main road leading to the park, at a cost of £l,lOO. Mr S Taylor said the Roads Board was giving £3OO towards this improvement, and the whole of the land was to be given by the owners with the exception of a strip for which the Council proposed to pay £lOO. The remaining £BOO, spread over thirty years, would only be equivalent to a rate of id. in the pound. Last year 11,074 motor-cars passed over the Transporter Bridge, and it was computed that fully two-thirds of these made use of the road, which was only 1211. 71n. wide in one part. Both sides of the road were elevated, and there was no escape from danger for women and children. Mr. Dean urged that it was ridiculous for the Council to spend £BOO on a partial improvement mainly for motorists. Dr.Nl'Donnell said the price suggested was exorbitant, though the improvement would be considerable. It was only for the benefit of motorists, who never spent sixpence in the town, but rushed through it. Mr. John Littler pointed out that the Council gave a tacit invitation to the children of the town to go to the park, and if they were injured !whilst going along a totally inadequate road the Council were responsible. Eventually the motion was carried.

CORONATION TABLET.

A handsome fumed oak tablet, presented by Sir John T. Brunner to commemorate the Coronation, has been placed in the Council Chamber since the last, meeting, and Mr. Poscett returned the Council's thanks. BOYS ON CANAL BOATS. ANOTHER DROWNING ACCIDENT.

At the Weaver Hotel, Weston Poiut, on Tuesday afternoon, Coroner Ridgway held an inquest on Fred Moores (fifteen), of CAnal-street, Runcorn, who fell from a canal boat, on Monday, and was drowned. The mother of the lad said he had occasionally fits of dizziness. Samuel Roughsedge (fifteen), llalton-road, Runcorn, said he and Moores worked on the canal boats Jane and Margaret. On Monday morning they were working their way with a load of slack up the Weaver to enter the Weston Canal. Witness went on ahead to open a lock gate. He heard Moores crack his whip as, a signal to the gateman. When witness turned round he saw his mate in the water. He ran and told the lock man. He thought Moores must have overbalanced when cracking the whip and fallen into the water. The Coroner said he did not know that it was advis- able for two boys to work these bouts, but Moores's father assured him they were quite able to do so. He thought, however, that when the boats were going through locks some older person should to in charge. The Coroner also commented upon the fact that Roughsedge, a lad of fifteen, was unable to read. The jury returned a verdict of" Acci dental death."

50 YEARS IN THE MINISTRY.

JUBILEE OF THE REV. JOHN GUNNELL.

On Sunday morning the pulpit of the Bold-street Wesleyan Church was occupied by the Ret,. J, Gunnell, who made reference to the fact that it was 50 years ago on that very day when he entered on his career as a Wesleyan minister. By the Conference of 1862, held in Cornwall, he was received into the ministry, and appointed the junior minister of the St. Just Circuit in West Cornwall, having as one of his colleagues the Rev. G. E. Young, who was Mr. Gunnell's immediate predecessor in the Warrington Cir cuit, and whose saintly memory is still held in the highest esteem by all who knew him. It is worthy to note that the Rev. William Booth and his devoted wife had just closed a great revival mission in the St. Just Wesleyan Chapel, which had made a profound impression on the neighbourhood and had swept hundreds of new converts into the society classes. Other missions were held in other places in the far West by that remarkable man and his wife, with like results. It was the beginning of General Booth's work as a great evangelist. Mr Gunnell's subsequent appointments were to Falmouth, Stonehouse, Newark Pontefract, Leeds, Bradford, Bingley, Blackpool, Sheffield and Warrington. He opened his commission as superin- tendent of the Warrington Circuit on the first Sunday in September. 1889, having as colleagues the Revs W Brookes and E H Jackson. A somewhat serious breakdown in health early in 1891 necessitated a rest of several months. Unfortunately the result of the illness was permanently impaired hearing. At the end of his three years' term be retired from The active ministry, became a supernumerary, and took up his residence in Penketh, where he and his good helpmeet have lived for the past 20 years. It is a matter of great thankfulness to Mr. Gunnell that he has been and still is able to render considerable pulpit and pastoral service to the various places on the Warrington Circuit. In the ordinary course of his 50 years' ministry he has preached 7,320 times, and more than 2,000 of these services have been conducted on this circuit.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON le.,,,clren. iff_ n-q

Photo THE REV. JOHN GUNNELL.

Many marked changes have taken place during these 23 years. The population of the town and suburbs has greatly increased. The churches are face to face with all the perplexing, si cial and moral pro- blems that are common to the great centres of industry. Many of our brave standard bearers have fallen. A new generation has sprung up, and is making new and urgent demands on the intelligence and zeal of the churches. We have little enough cause for boasting, still it is giatifying to note the steady, substantial progress the Warrington Circuit has made during the past quarter of a century. In 1889 the total church membership was 1,060. To-day it is over 1,200. Then there were but three ministers, now there are four and three supernumeraries. New chapels have been built in Latchford, Stockton Heath, and Ellesmere-street, new schools at Bold- street, Moore, Padgate, Sankey Bridges, and Elles- mere street : and considerable enlargements of chapel ,or school or both, at Penketh and Padgate ; while a site has been purchased and plans prepared for a new school chapel in Orford. In all this Mr Gunnell sees abundant reason for thankfulness, and strong incen- tives to more diligent and faithful toil in the ensuing years. In that toil he hopes for at least a little while to take some small part. We are sure !that Mr Gunnell's many friends and admirers in the War. ringion circuit will warmly endorse that hope.

Wycliffe ES A. Society.

There was a good attendance on Sunday presided over by Mr NV Beswick (vice president). Mr It Wood give the address on " What it is to be a Christian," not simply a professing Christian or a member of a church, but one who lived a life according to the pattern of Christ. Mr NV H Payton rendered an organ 8010. Mr C Cotton read the lesson and the chairman led the prayer. Six new members received a hearty welcome into the brother- hood. Special reference was made to the late General Booth and his work for the uplifting of the poor and hopeless.

Brick-street Mission.

The above mission held their anniversary sermons on Sunday. Mr Rushton Silson, of Wigan being the preacher. In the afternoon the Emission choir gave a service of song entitled " The Elder Brother." Mr B Farrar occupied the chair and took the part of reader in the service in a most pleasing and interesting manner which was greatly appreciated by those present. The entire programme was gone through in a very satis- factory manner under the leadership of Mr Wm Barton, sen. Mr Tomkinson officiated at the organ. At the evening service the mission choir rendered two special anthems in excellent style and deserve special credit, Miss H Howell taking the solo. The collection for the day realised between £7 and £B.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John's Presbyterian Church.

The Rev. W. Reid occupied his pulpit at St. John's on Sunday evening last, after an absence of several Sabbaths. The parable of the mirror or metal reflector of St. Paul's day, was the topic of the sermon wherein the preacher proceeded to contrast the perfect with the imperfect world of knowledge. After pleading for an open mind as the only ono suitable to a world of imperfect yet progressive knowledge, he showed that what is but imperfectly seen is not necessarily unreli- able, and that many things can only be seen by reflec- tion, illustrating by methods used in connection with the recent eclipse of the sun. Only in this way could they at present know God or even their friends. Reflected on the mirror of countenance or attitude they saw all they were able of their real life. The smallest hand-glass could take in the sun's disc, and the most insignificant life could reflect the life of God, but to do so the mirror must be clean.

The New Congregational School-Chapel Padgate Lane. Wycliffe Congregational Church magazine contains the following :—Permit me to supplement the efforts of the Treasurer and Secretaries of the above fund by an appeal for urgent and immediate action. The work at Cockhedge Mission is suffering more than any of our Wycliffe people can realize, and is the cause of serious anxiety to the committee and workers. In the present, buildings the congregation cannot be sustained much' longer. The falling away from time to time is deplor- able, and cannot be supplemented by others. The •Sunday School continues prosperous. but it is with grave feelings of responsibility that the officials view the future. rhe general verdict of all workers is that the larger room is totally unfit for further use, when the wet and wintry season is upon us. We shudder to think of the experiences of last winter (when we many times questioned the wisdom of allowing the children to meet in a room with walls, floor and roof, saturated with water). What can we do, with over 200 scholars, and the problem of having to resort to a smaller room for all our meetings ? Those who under- stand the work and the need for many classes, will recognize the impossibility of our task. We love our Sunday school and the scholars love it too, and any restriction of work will be painful to all concerned. But we still cling to the hope that it will be possible to raise a sufficient amount to justify an early com- mencement of the new premises. The splendid lead given by the individual subscribers of nearly £l,OOO is worthy of appreciation and immediate action. Surely the splendid work that has been sustained for so many years at Cockhedge, should not be allowed to further suffer by an undue delay, especially when the accom- plishment of the scheme is within reach.—Yours sincerely, F. MORRALL. -

Wycliffe Congregational Church. The Rev. B. Nightingale, M.A.' of Preston, chairman of the Lancashire Congregational Union and the author of a history of Nonconformity in the County, preached morning and evening in Wycliffe Congregational Church •on Sunday. In an address to the young people at the morning service Mr. Nightingale referred to the recent visit of the King to Lancaster, and said one of the most interesting ceremonies was the presentation by the Constable, of the keys of the castle to His Majesty. One of these keys had been in use since the Elizabethan period. The castle was specially interesting to Non- conformity from the fact that some of their ancestors were incarcerated there rather than deny what they ' held to be true and opposed to religious liberty. In a delightful manner the preacher pleaded with the children to give Jesus Christ the key to the castle of their hearts. The sermon was based on the words " But when the morning was now come Jeans stood on the shore" (John xxi. 4). The preacher pointed out that whilst Christ's ministry on earth was a public one the few instances recorded of his appearances after His resurrection were entirely concerned with Himself and his disciples. While his first ministry was a public one, his second was pre- eminently to His own disciples, His idea being to Equip them for their great work of going forth to the world with His message. Whatever might be said about tbe Church not being democratic to-day—a charge he was not prepared to admit—it was clear that the common People heard Christ gladly, and it was the duty of His followers to-day to go forth and preach the Gospel to all nations. The evening discourse was based on the text " For every man shall bear his own burden." (Galatians Cc., riv.) He pointed out that the besetting sin of most people was dependence ; it was easier to lean on others than to go alone. A man was invariably the better for being thrown upon his own resources. It was this fact which reconciled us to many of life's trials and showed us a beneficient purpose shining through them. There was never a time when there was greater need for the remembrance of this truth than to-day. The chief characteristic of our day was its collectiveism. This was seen in legislation, in religion and in industrial life. The preacher proceeded to show that it was the indiyidual who really counted, and that he must bear his own burden. They conld not lose the individual in the State, in society or in the Church without taking away from man his most precious heritage, and reducing him to a mere fraction of responsibility and conscienceless abstraction. A man's own burden WNS inseparable from his life. Howaver much we might try to pass duty and responsibility on to others, we knew that the burden of responsibility was our own. and however poor and weak we might be, God had given us power to live strong, brave and useful lives. 'The ordinance of the Lord's Supper was afterwards observed, presided over by Mr. Nightingale. The pulpit will be occupied to-morrow (Sunday) by the Rev. G. E. Cheeseman, of Huyton, who will after- wards speak at Cairo-street Church at the united meet- ing of the Free Churches to commemorate the 250th anniversary of the ejectment of the Nonconforming Ministers from the Church of England in 1672. A Warrior Bold. The General is dead, long live the General !William Booth (says the Buttonwood Parish Magazine) has passed upon his way, William Bramwell Booth has stepped into the breach. As the founder of that now vast , organisation, the Salvation Army, William Bramwell Booth never can be forgotten, his name must be included in the roll of the world's greatest benefactors. The grand old man was a warrior bold, weilding the sword right up to the end. While he lived there could be, to use his own words, 'only one General." He was the beloved autocrat of all the Salvationists, he was a unique example of successful autocracy. Yet he owed much.. so much, in earlier days to his revered wife's inspiration, influence and judgment. How greatly indebted the Army is to the unconscious influence or a saintly woman the General alone could tell. Great man, as undoubtedly he was, General Booth the second may prove an even more successful warrior than his xenowned father. " The General has laid down his sword." His son has taken it up, and girded it on. God buries his worker, but carries on -His work. Talking in Church.

It is always interesting to the Clergy (writes the Rev H B Firth in St James' Parish Magazine) when on their holidays to go to church on Sundays as members of the congregation and not int their official capacity. I had the pleasure and privilege of attending several well-known Churches on the South Coast during the month of August and was particularly struck by the silence of the congregation before the commencement of the services. There was a remarkable absence of whispering and semi-audible conversation which is terribly distracting and quite out of place in the House of God. Rlany people suppose that they are at liberty to whisper and converse in low tones in Church before the beginning of service, but this is really to treat the Church as though it were a concert room where of course there is nothing objec- tionable in people chatting to each other betore the performance begins. There is something very impressive about the stillness which should precede Divine Service; it denotes the ieverent, expectant attitude of , the worshippers bringingtto one's mind the words cf the Prophet Habakkuk, "The Lord is in His holy temple: let all the earth keep silence before Him." Church Teaching in Secondary Schools. The Warrington Parish Church magazine has some notes on the "right of Church parents so request Instruction in Church doctrine for their children attending secondary schools." The writer says :—lt seems not to be generally known that Church of England parents have this right. It is all important therefore to call attention to it and to press them to exercise it. In regard to secondary schools, aided but not provided by the local education authority, the regulations run : "If the instrument under which the school is governed requires or does not prohibit the giving in the school of religious instruction, distinctive of any particular denomination, the governors may provide such instruction for any pupil upon the written request of the parent or guardian of the pupil," with the proviso that " such instruction, if given, must be provided by funds other than grants made by the Board of Education or any local authority." In regard to secondary schools provided by the local authority, the Education Act of 1902, Section IV., provides," In such schools, no catechism or formulary, distinctive of any religious denomination, shall be taught, except in cases when the Council, at tile request of parents or schclars, at such times and under such conditions as the Council think desirable, allow any religious instruction to be i given in the school, college or hostel, otherwise than at the cost of the Council." It will be seen from these two quotations that the parents ha7e the right to apply for Church instruction for the children attending secondary schools with the certainty of its being complied with in the case of the non-provided schools and the probability of its being so granted in the case of the provided schools As regards the cost, not a great deal, that could be met by the subscriptions of those concerned or by the diocesan organization for the purpose. In most cases some Church teachers on the staff would be willing to give the instruction. It is so done in many secondary schools throughout the country. It is to be hoped that Church parents will rouse themselves to a sense of their duty in this respect, and try to make provision for the Church teaching of their children. So many of our boys and girls in secondary schools are growing up in complete ignorance of the principles end doctrines of their Church that it behoves us to do all we can to bring that knowledge within their reach. It is surpris- ing what a large proportion of them do not even know their catechism. in some cases they have never seen it. It is easy to see how little prepared they are for confirmation, and how slack must be their hold upon , their Church. Let the parents take counsel with the vicar of the parish in which the school is situated and common action can be arranged.

Bazaars—" Christian Barbarisms."

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell has the following in the " Burtonwood Parish Magazine " :—Much as we dis- like bazaars, deeming them Christian barbarisms and religious atrocities, we are unwilling to believe the truth of the following, as we understand, authentic and uncontradicted note which was recently published in our daily papers :—" Here's a dreadful business. It is alleged that ladies who help at bazarrs also help them- selves and make a respectable amount of pin money ' 'by rendering incorrect accounts of their sales. Sir George Alexander says the practice has become perfectly scandalous' in extent. But what can be expected of a method of raising money that blackmails tradesmen, compels people to buy what they don't want at double its value, encourages gambling, develops the feminine gift of persuasive invention, and generally demoralises everyone who has anything to do with it ?" We have ourselves heard of stall-holders departing homewards at night-time with arms full of unpurchased wares for which no payment had been proffered but we have forborne to criticise, feeling some reasonable explanation might be forthcoming which would put a different complexion on doings obviously suspicious. Doings of this " perfectly scandalous" type accentuate the demand for the banning of bazaars tor furtherance of religious work. Let bazaar mongers cast the first stone at dishonest tradesmen—if they can.

A Tribute to General Booth.

" St. Paul's Parish Magazine" says : During the past month we feel we have lost by death a very great man, known to us as General Booth. Although he was not a member of the Church of England, and with some of his methods perhaps we are not all agreed, neverthe- less, we feel the country has lost a good man, and we all feel the poorer for his death. It seems to me the great secret of his success was in his simple faith in God, his wonderful belief in prayer, and his dogged perseverance in spite of opposition. These traits in his character we do well to imitate and though we cannot all be General Booths,still,if we rely upon the strength be sought, we should all do a great deal more than we are doing. We can only hope and pray that his spirit may rest upon his successor, and that the work he began and carried on may be continued and increased under the new regime. We mourn with those who particularly mourn, and we thank God for the great work he accomplished.

The Future of the Church House.

The future of the Church House, Brigde-street, is discussed at some length in the Warrington Parish Church Magazine and the necessity of raising an annual income of £lOO is urged. With a further moderate expenditure the usefulness of the Church House could be greatly increased ; its situation is central and con- venient, but there is a serious drawback in the noise of the street traffic. To obviate this double windows could be put into the large room at a cost of £l6, of which amount the landlord has kindly promised to pay one half. Three more rooms could be rented, which would enable a room to be reserved exclusively for ladies ; a tea room, and a room for an attendant. The house would then serve the purpose of a club. Ladies could send their parcels there, have tea and write letters or meet their friends. With an attendant always present it would not be necessary to lock the rooms as at present. The usefulness of the house would be greatly increased. It is estimated that the extra cost of this development would be about .£6O, but it is hoped that there would be a material increase in the number of subscribing members. It is suggested by the committee that a source of income might be provided by the clergy and churchwardens in the neighbourhood devoting one whole Sunday's offertories towards the work end maintenance of the ,1 Church House, which has already been acknowledged by many of the clergy as a great centre for Church work and an important asset for I the whole Church. It is, therefore, urged by the committee that the maintenance of the Warrington Church House should be very seriously con- sidered, and that a work for the Church once begun should not be laid aside, neglected and forraken. We enclose a letter from the Biihop of Liverpool, in which he commends the usefulness and importance of the con- tinuance of the Church House to your earnest con- sideration: 'lt would be a great misfortune if the • Warrington Church House were cicsed. It has a dis- tinct part to play in the Church life of the neighbour- hood as a centre for the transaction of Church business and for the holding of meetings and conferences. If it could also be extended so as to serve the purpose of a. club, it would meet a real need and its usefulness would be greatly increased. I shall be very happy to become a member. "

The Distress in Norwich.

Sir,—On behalf of the London Society of East Anglians we venture to appeal for donations to the Fund opened by this Society for the alleviation of the dire distress and misery now existing in the city of Norwich and the surrounding districts. Funds are badly needed at once, for the damage and loss caused by the catastrophe is in no way exaggerated. The local resources cannot cope with this heavy misfortune, and our Society desire to help the thousands of homeless sufferers who are unable to help themselves. We ask for the prompt assistance of every lover of East Anglia, and in particular we appeal to all East Anglians and to those who treasure pleasant memories of our old-world city, her famous Broads and seaside resorts. Donations, however small, will be gratefully received by the Honorary Secretary, Captain H. D. Barnes, 17, John-street, Bedford-row, Loudon, W.C. We are, Sir, your obedient servants, NORFOLK, President. R. S. CHANDLER, Chairman. W. B. HOWLS, Vice-Chairman. September 2nd, 1912.

Paltry Progress.

Sir,—Your two correspondents take up attitudes which are so divided as to make it impossible to answer both in one letter. I therefore crave the indulgence of Mr. Baguley whilst I try to clear up the difficulties which our friend" L.0.V." has asked me to deal with. I ask Mr. Baguley to grant this favour because the points raised in his article are so important that to do justice to them demands pointed replies rather than mere references. lam pleased to have interested" LOX." and hope also to convince him of the need of strikes, as a necessary weapon in class conflicts. In my letter (f last week I stated " The strike roust be recognised as a means of economic readjustment, in reply to which "L 0.V." says he fails to discover where I prove the strike to remedy economic inequality." These are two totally different positions. The strike is a means not the remedy. Now I wish to state that these questions which go right to the root of political economy can only be understood by a little menial exertion and careful investigation. Capitalism is a complex organization, its laws are difficult to under- stand, consequently in justice to readers we must deal carefully with the subject. " LOX." claims that my illustration of last week, where I say increased prices and competitions for jobs induce men to organize for better conditions, proves that the economic factor is against the worker. that is true, but it does not prove the stiike to be impotent. Before proceeding further I wartt, to make reference to a misconstruction which if allowed to pass would appear as a contradiction. Later on " L.0.V." says I admit that the constant rise in prices must reduce wages to the lowest subsistence level. This I must contradict and also point out that no Socialist with a knowledge of Mani= economics ever admitted it. The fact is,if the worker's wage remained fixed, whilst prices rise, then the worket s would be reduced to a bare subsistence level. The strike prevents it. Wha, "LOX." and many others fail to see is that a 'ise of wages follows a rise of price and not that a rise of price always follows a rise of wages. This does not appear a real difference at first sight. The feeble attempts of the workers to-day to better their condi- tions always follow a rise in prices. What does this prove? It proves that the workers are com:oelled by force of circumstances to strike, and 90 win back by direct action what they have lest by the operation of economic factors. "L.O V." says in effect that the Capitalists can at will raise prices. This is a fallacy, every bankrupt proves it. Why resist a rise of wages if the Capitalist can raise prices ! Why not give labourers :£2 per week and raise price 100 per cent. Every time a workers' represeutative asks for an increase from the employer for the man, he is told that the employer cannot afford and that it would prevent him meeting his competitors in the open market, clearly proving that the capitalist cannot fix price. Now if the strike enables toe workers to win back what they have lost by increased prices surely the same method can procure for him additional increases. If goods rise 10 per cent, and to meet that the worker makes his wage if 18s. into £1 then why without a rise in prices cannot he obtain 255. Prices are determined by the cost in labour power of gold. Wages are determined by the cost of men and the power of organization. The worker, unfortunately, acts instinctively, he thee not understand what means, When he realises how labour conquers everything the strike will be used aggres- sively rather than in defence. "LOX." says further that the standard of life depends upon the amount and quality of commodities we receive for our wage, not upon the amount of wage i we receive. He says, n effect, what the worker wantsl is not _4:2 per week but £2 worth of goods for the ,+:1 he receives. The standard of life measured in money is quantitatively determined and no amount of tautology can alter the fact. Money is a commodity, and the more you have of it the better off you are. The worker wants wages increasing until he has absorbed profits and at that point the wages system is negated. Let me re-state the position. Prices continue to rise as gold cheapens, so that the purchasing power of the pound becomes less. Labour unorganized fails to Increase wages correspondingly, with the result that " real wages" fall. A rise of wages takes places after a rise in prices have created economic discontent. This is only instinctive resistance. Industrial organization, or the new industrial unionism, has for its object the abolition of the wages system. This can only be accomplished by increasing wages to a point where capital expansion is impossible.— Yours &c., CHARLES DUKES. Stockton Heath, Warrington.

THE WOMAN & THE DOCTOR.

Doctors say that if men bad to bear as mach pain as women, there would not be enough medical men to take care of them. But woman—almost every woman —is a heroine. If the only alternative were a medical examination there would be an excuse, if not a good reason,for seek- ing to conceal these ailments. Fortunately there is no necessity to suffer. A COUNCIL SCENE.

INSURANCE ACT CRITIC WHO "KNOWS NOTHING ABOUT IT."

Alderman Bolton has to Withdraw.

There was a heated" scene" at the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening, following a bitter attack upon the Insurance Act by Alderman Bolton who, while describing the measure as " the biggest swindle of the century," confessed that he knew nothing about it. The Mayor warmly deprecated this introduction of political partizanship in the proceedings of the Council and when the Alderman went on to declare that the Act was a fraud upon the community, his Worship insisted upon a withdrawal of the expression. For a time Alderman Bolton refused to withdraw, but after a very lively passage-at-arms, he eventually deferred to the ruling of the chair. The breeze arose on the recommendation of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee that tbe Insurance Committee should be allowed the use of Town Hall at a charge of 2s. per meeting. I wish to say that, as a political partisan, Alderman Bolton, in the proper place and on a suitable occasion, has a perfect right to ventilate his opinions with regard to the Act, and to say snytbing he likes.

Alderman 13,,LT0N : Mr. Mayor, I was talking about letting the Town Hall at 2s. per meeting. (To Coun- cillor Roberts) : Don't criticise me.

Councillor itosEnrs : I am not going to criticise you. Alderman BOLTON : lam not going to allow you to criticise me.

The MAIon (to Alderman Bolton) : I think you ought to be quiet. Alderman BOLTON' : Well, I have been until you asked me to withdraw that.

Councillor Roams : I think I am justified in saying that the Alderman has confused the Act itself with the administration of it, which is an entirely different thing. There is the time and place for criticism of the Act, but this is neither the time nor the place. The Act is in operation and it is part of the duty of this community, through the proper channel, to administer the Act to the best of its ability. The committee entrusted with that duty, con- tinued Councillor Roberts, was a composite one and only recently the Council elected a large proportion of representatives on it. If the Council thought it was consistent with its dignity to refuse the use of the Town Hall—a public building—for the purpose of assisting in the administration of an Act of Parlia- ment (Alderman BOLTON : If it had been for nothing 1 I would not have objected)—or if any member thought so, he should be sorry for him, or for the Council should it arrive at such a decision. There was not, and could not possibly be, any taint of party about the administration of the Act. That the Act might require amendment every one would admit, but that was outside the scope of that discussion. The Council had to consider whether in its sense of obliga- tion to public duty it would provide facilities for the proper administration of the Act. He was surprised that the committee had not thought it fitting and consistent with their views upon the question 'of carrying out public duty to allow the use of the room without any charge. Alderman BOLTON: I have no objectien to that. If you strike cut the 2s I will support it. Councillor WILKINSON : You are throwing money away. Alderman BOLTON : I don't believe in knobsticking. Send it back and we will let them have it for nothing. Councillor RonERTs : Alderman Bolton and I are in agreement on one matter, and that is that it is not dignified--(Alderman Bolton : That's it:)—for this Corporation to take the sum of 2s. per night from the Insurance Committee—

The AIAYort: It may be for the lighting and that kind of thing. Councillor ROBERTS : It is not an adequate pay- ment. If the Insurance Committee has to make an adequate payment, let it be one that it is worth the white of the Corporation to collect and consistent

with services rendered. Until we, as the Insurance Committee, on which this Council is largely repre- sented, have an opportunity of seeing what income and expenditure there are likely to be, it would be a graceful thing and consistent with public duty reflecting some credit on this Council, that the com- mittee should he permitted the use of one of the rooms in this ball for the administration of the Act. He moved, as an amendment, that the tninut-s be con- firmed with the exception of the resolution relating to the use of the Town Hall. Alderman BouroN : I will second that with the greatest of pleasure. I object to the committee being so undignifiPd as to attempt to charge 2s. The MAYOR : You secoud that.

Alderman BOLTON : No. I second this amendment. It would be more dignified to let them have the use of the room free than by making ounelves common knobstickers._

Alder man TINNION said it was illogical fora member of the Council to propose or second an amendment to his own resolution.

Councillor HAW-THOR \ : As we seem to be in a dilemma about it, I will second the amendment.

Alderman BoLrox then agreed to withdraw the paragraph for reconsideration by the committee. He said the Insurance Committee could have the Town Hall for nothing, as far as he was concerned. They had had it free before, and he did not see why they should not have it again.

Councillor WILKIKSON : Hear, Lear. lam glad to see you take that attitude. The minute was withdrawn and the incident closed.

ECHO OF LIBERAL WOMEN'S TRIP.

Presentation to Rescuer.

A sequel to the life-saving incident at Latchford Locks, on the occasion of the annual trip of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association, was witnessed at the Liberal Club on Tuesday evening, when a presentation was made to the hero cf that memorable day, Samuel Ashworth, of the steamship " Claughton." The affair was promoted by the Women's Liberal Association and the popularity of the event was evidenced by the exceedingly large cumber who attended. The club was barely adequate to accommodate the assembly and we understand that, if the commit:oe had thought such interest would have been evinced, the Co-operative flail would have been secured, and even that would have been filled. Mrs Osborne and her committee of willing workers had done all in their power to make the presentation a success and we feel sure they felt well satisfied with the response that was made to their appeal. Prior to the presentation the committee sat down to tea and subsequently an adjournment was made to the billiard roorn where the event of the evening took place. Mr. E. Osborne presided and he was supported I y Mrs Osborne (secretary of the association), Captain W. home and Chief Officer Mayes (of tie Clatightorib and othe s. The t IRNIAN said it was ahi ays a pleasure to be at any function orliat.ised by ttte Warrington Wonien's Liberal Association. Everything they did was always successful and that night what wwi intended as a quiet, inform ii gathering had res,Th•erl itself into a huge over-flow meeting. (Hear, he tr ) They were .all glad to be prssent to honour the brave deed

remember this occasion and the genuine appreciation of your heroic act by the Liberal Women of Warrington. (Applause). The clock is a very handsome one of inlaid mahogany and has a full chime. It is inscribed as follows ; "Presented to Mr. Samuel Ashworth by the Warrington Women's Liberal Asscciation in recogni- ti, nof his bt every in rescuing a man from Latchford Locks, July 30th, 1912." Mr. Ashworth was also the ' recipient ISf a pair of fine bronze side ornaments. Mr. ASIIWORTII, who appeared somewhat taken aback by the handsome recognition of his conduct, could only say a few words, thanking them all very much for the magnificent present. He only did what any other human being would do, to save a fellow creature's life ; because life was vety precious to anyone. (Applause). Captain ROME was pleas.d to be there on the occasion of a presentation to a member of his crew. He related a thrilling story of the incident at Latchford Locks and said that after an experience of over 30 years, he could testify to the bi avery of the Woodside Ferry-men, not one but all of their. On occasions when the boat had been going at full speed up the middle of the river, men had jumned over-beard and rescued people from drowning. That was not the first time Mr. Ashworth had rescued a fellow creature and the captain related stories of former brave deeds done by his seaman. Those who were present on the night of the rescue would remember when coming up the locks, how dark it was, and vv hen they had entered the lock there was a sudden cry of "Man overboard." It was a terrible shock to every- one present,but again they heard that one of the sailors had jumped between the boat and the wall of the lock, then came the cry "He has got him." Both men were raised from the murky pool to the lock above, and they: were so excited that night and so delighted to find that the man had been rescued, that scarcely one of them thought of extending thanks to the brave man who had saved his life. They had invited Mr. Ashworth that night because he was a hero—(hear. hear and applause)—and to thank him personally and publicly for the brave action he accomplished on that occasion. (Applause.) They had hoped Mrs Dodds would make the presentation, but unfortunately a sudden bereavement prevented her being present. He therefore called on the Secretary of the Women's Liberal Association to make the presentation. Mrs OSBORNE made a neat little speech in present- ing Mr Ashworth with the timepiece and bronzes. To Commemorate the Great Ejectment.

As will be seen from our advertising columns a united meeting of Free-Churchmen is to be held 'in Cairo-street Chapel to-morrow (Sunday) evening at 8 o'clock to commemorate the stand taken for Scripture and constitution by the ejected clergy of the Estab- lished Church of England 250 years ago. The meeting will be devotional, and if, as he fears, the Rev. John Yonge will not be able to preside, his place will be taken by the Rev. J. J. Glover of Rislet. Other ministers will take part, including the Rev. G. E. Cheeseman, who will deliver the address.

DRIVEN TO THE WORKHOUSE. After serving twelve years in the Grenadier Guards and four years in the East Surrey Regiment and going through the Boer War, a man of 40 years of age who came before the Huntingdon Board of Guardians said that he had to go into the workhouse. He is a tailor by trade, and the workhouse master said that he could give him some repairing to do for a time.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Charles T. Aldrich is a great entertainer, and one well understands the reasons which secured his appearance at the command performance before the King and Queen in July. As a" man of many parts" he stands unrivalled on the music hall stage, and his visit to Warrington tbia week has been a great treat to patrons of the Friars Green House. Probably the most remarkable of Aldrich's many notable achieve- ments is in the quick-change line of business. How he does it is the marvel of the moment. To see him as David Garrick in silken hose, ruffle and doublet, and watch him dash round the screen and re-appear in the false nose, whiskers, and shappy " slops" of Fagin the Jew, compels one's admiration—as does every other quick change in Aldrich's repertoire. His absence from view cannot be more than five seconds, and yet the metamorphosis is complete in every detail. Then as the tramp juggler Aldrich is supremely diverting and mysterious, while his burlesque magic as " Wun Lung Foo " is smartness itself. Aldrich is a wonderful man, and there is all the American hustle in everything he does. The audience would have been well content if he had been the only turn. But there are other items in an exceptional bill. Jack Lane, the Yorkshire rustic, provokes roars of laughter with his broad humour and eccentricites at the piano. Thornbury, the lightning painter and entertainer, contrives to hold the interest and stimulate the risibillties of the audience by his smart work with pencil, chalk, andi soot. Some smart simultane- ous dancing s provided by Doris and Fracces, but they would be well advised to drop the vocal part of the turn. Singing is not their forte. May Poultonhas some good songs, and 8 comedienne.hl n 5style. ThrShi lel ai as quite above re the main cling balancing act by the Wood- wards.averageof themthe The lady dces some uncommonly smart gymnastics on a cycle mounted on a lofty ladder which is balanced solely on the shoulder of her sturdy male companion. Stanley Thorn is a great success as a light chorus comedian, and his song "Eh, what, rather," brings down the house. " Three Singing Colliers" collaborate in a song scena "Home from the Pit," which we thought the least attractive item. Their style of part-singing could be much improved. Other turns will be provided by Cole and Rags, comedy jugglers; Maurin's marvellous Fountains, a wonderful stage spectacle; Dot Hardy, comedienne t Great Scott, the ladder king ; Billy Simpson, Australian character comedian ; and Rosa Fitzgerald, chic ccmedienne.

LIBERAL MEETING IN HOWLEY.

ALDERMAN BOLTON'S " CRITICISM " OF THE INSURANCE ACT.

Linder the auspices of the Warrington Liberal Asso- ciation a well-attended meeting was held on vacant land near Ellesmere-street in the Howley Ward on Wednesday evening. Mr M Llewellyn presided and the principal speaker was Councillor James Kendall, of Manchester.

At the outset the Chairman said we were in a period of record•making in regard to legislation. They believed that the progressive legislation of the present Government was for the sole benefit of the workingrnen. (Hear, bear.) There had never been a Government in power which had done so much for the benefit of the workingolass. As was always the case when progress was being made, they had been misrepresented and pulled to pieces, but he believed that the weight of criticism was beginning to turn against those who so unjustly criticised. Their opponents had told them that if Home Rule were granted to Ireland, there would be war and bloodshed, but such talk would die down now and the opposition to the Home Rule Bill would sink into oblivion, like all the other criticisms. (Hear, hear.) The Irishmen were men with brains and would well know how to govern tnemselves successfully and prosperously. A W.ONDERFUL SCENE.

There had been an alderman of the borough criticising the Insurance Act in a very strong manner and after saying very hard things about it he said "1 know nothing about it." (Laughter). He thought that man was a most wonderful person. (Laughter and applause.) Councillor KENDALL gave an interesting address on the programme of the Liberal Party and spoke of the great service they had rendered the working class population during the last six years. The working class homes were being made better and they would be brightened still more in the fature,as a result of legisla- tion of a Liberal character. (Applause'. Perhaps the most important thing Liberalism had done was to stand as a bulwark against that iniquitous policy of the Tory Party, tariff reform. One of the great debts the people owed to the Liberal Party was, that when they came in office in lON, there was a very serious menace of tariff reform becoming an accomplished part of the machinery of the country, but the Liberals established a Government which was gradually driving tariff reform into the background. They could never have a system of tariff reform and the goods be cheaper. (Hear, hear.) If there were a system of tariff reform in operation, the cost of living to the working- man would be far greater and as a result the family mi nu would be less and they could never expect phyei- cally fit children if they had to reduce the food in the home. it was the first plank in the platform of the Tory party and then they bcasted of being the friends of the working class. ("Shame.") All the countries that were existing under the horrible system, were anxious to free themselves of so unjust an imposition of taxer. The Liberal Party was the one for the work- ingmen and they should giva it all the support they could. (Applause.) Mr E 1(1, YLINE-JONES referred to the statement of Alderman Bolton in the Town Council that " the Incur- ' ance Act was the biggest swindle of the century."
