Just imagine a man who, according to his own admis- sion, knew nothing at all about it, describing a great social measure as "a big swindle." Liberals, Lauour- men and Tories united in saying that a scheme of National Insurance was en absolute necessity and we all wondered how in the world we had been able to get on without such a scheme in the past. The Insurance Act was going to put a great lever into the hands of local authorities for dealing with consumption. Not only would an attempt be made to cure consumption, but by National Insurance the autholities would be helped to sweep away the plague spots which created consumption. By National Insurance, we were going to save thousands of blighted homes and broken hearts. It was going t 3 do more for the social welfare of the people than any other Act had done, because it was not simply a palliative, but it great meaeure striking at the roots of she social problem. GENERAL BOOTH MEMORIAL.

New Leader Suggests Army Training College.

General Bramwell Booth, the new head of the Salvation, Army, has issued a communication to the Press regarding a projected memorial to his late father. Nothing would have been further from the late General's desires, he says than a tribute expressed in stone or iron. The project he had deeply in thought—but he hesitated to ask for the large sum required—was an institution for the more complete training and equipment of officers than had hitherto been possible, and the new chief thinks this would be the most lasting and effective memorial. For this he asks £150,000 for buildings, salaries, and upkeep, and he promises that not a shilling shall be spent in turrets or marble ornamentation, but simple suitable, sanitary buildings shall be erected "GREATEST MAN OF HIS CENTURY."

The Free Trade Hall, Manchester, was crowded on Thursday night with fully 5,000 persons at the service of the Salvation Army in memory of the late General Booth, at which the new leader, General Bramwell Booth, and Mrs Booth spoke. The Lord Mayor of Manchester presided, and with him on the platform were Dean Welldon, the president-designate of the Wesleyan Conference (the Rev S F Collier), and many well-known local public men and religious workers. The Lord Mayor spoke of the late General, as the greatest man nf his century.

===== 0002601_19120907_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRIKGTON'S TRIBUTE TO THE LATE GENERAL.

Warrington did homage to the memory of the late Chief of the Salvation Army on Sunday evening when a memorial service was held at the Hippodrome where, only a few months ago, General Booth addressed his last meeting in the town. The spacious building was placed at the disposal of the Salvation Army by Mr. Frank Macnaghten the managing director, free of charge. Bewsey-street presented a crowded appear- ance about 7 o'clock when a huge procession was formed near the Farmer's Stores, headed by the Salvation Army Band, Adjutant and Mrs. William F.mwick. in charge of the corps, and Adjutants Gill, Caradine and Christy of the Fernhead Home of Rest, and marched slowly by way of Horsemarket-street and Bridge-street to the Hippodrome. A large number joined the precession en route and at the service there was an attendance of about 2,000 people. The Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph) attended to pay his tribute to the late General and spoke in very appreciative terms of his long and useful career. Sympathetic allusion was made to the death of Mrs Robert Garnett, of Penketh, at whose house the General stayed on his last visit to Warrington. Alder- men Bennett read the messages which had been sent to the international headquarters by the king and Queen, Queen Alexandra and the Kaiser, and also gave his impressions of the funeral of the General. Prayers having been offered by Adjutant Christy and Mrs Gill, the huge congregation joined in the singing of the hymn " Above the waves of earthly strife." The band contributed appropriate selections, including Songs of comfort." Adjutant Caradine having read a portion of Scripture, Adjutant Fenwick gave an address, based on the words, " What bath God wrought !" lie not only dealt with the great work which the late General had accomplished by the help of the Almighty, and his wonderful powers of organization, but instanced the case of a Warrington man who had been "converted " by means of the services of the Salvation Army, and was now a far better man. He and his wife wore the uniform of the army, and the children attended the Sunday school. The hymn " I can never repay," was sung, and Adjutant Fenwick pronounced the Benediction.

WARRINGTON INFIRMARY AND DISPENSARY. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that a Special General Meeting of Governors of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary will be held at the Infirmary on Friday, the 27th September,l9l2, at Three o'clock in the afternoon for the following purposes :- I.—To appoint an Honorary Assistant Surgeon. 2.—To consider the following recommendation by the Board of Management, and to take such action thereon as may be thought fit :—" That rule so be amended to read as follows :—They shall, from their number, appoint nine House Visitors, who shall also act as a Finance Com- mittee. In appoiPting these members six shall be selected from the General Board of M nage- ment ; one from the Medical Board, and two from the representatives of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee on the Board of Manage- ment. Three shall form a quorum.'" The Board of Management invite applications for the position of Honoray Assistant Surgeon. Candidates must be registered to practice Medicine and Surgery. No medical gentleman is eligible unless in practice within the County Borough of Warrington. Rules and duties may be bad on application. Applications for the position may be sent to the Secretary on or before Monday,the 23rd September,l9l2 J. H. J. biAMPSON, Secretary. Dated September 7th, 1912.

Warrington started against the sun and wind and the home teem were the first to attack, but a fine response was forthcoming from the two inside men and Wrigley and company were not allowed to travel far. 'the Warrington forwards soon showed their nippiness by rushing the ball to the other end. Here Rushton got the ball out to Brooks and the visiting backs made a strong movement towards the home line. Bradshaw received, but was injured in a tackle and had to retire from the game when it was five minutes old, with a strained shoulder. This was cruel luck for Warrington. who had to take Tranter from the pack. Huddersfield soon showed that the advantage of numbers was considerable and Jolley had a warm time on defence. His first clear- ance was a failure, but afterwards the young full back ran, punted and tackled in flco style. The home lot handled smartly, and Rosenfeld got up speed on the wing, but Renwick cut across and downed his fellow-

Every man on the Leigh oide has been announced fit, and so the directors are able to play the following representative team S Johnson : Neville, Tom Johnson (capt.), Roberts, Donohue; Cain, Mooney; Lee, Lowe, J Cartwright, Melling, Davies, and Barton. Reserve half: M`Geiver. , By the way, Arnold had the honour of scoring the first try of the season for Warrington, and very smartly be did it. He and Skelhorar were exception- ally prominent in the loose, and set a lively pace at times. -- Thomas signed on on Wednesday, but as the teams were selected, he will have to wait a week before ha resumes active warfare. He is keen to begin, and proper attention to training vat make his place secure. Slackness sill assuredly bring suspension. The Committee mean to use the iron hand this season. Mclntyre has not set signed. He had better be getting into harness, for there are some keen youngsters with an eye on promotion. But " Mac " can make himself 'a cert " if be tries. Jack G Mo.we is the second capture Warrington made during the week end. Moore is a Cumberland County centre, and last season played with., Batley under an agreement by vkhich he was free to leave if Me wished. According to the " Athletic News" 'Moore is a fine footballer and worth much more than the £lO Batley wet() compelled to release him for. Be will turn out for the "A " team to-day. A photo of Moore in County jersey and cap appears herewith. By profession he is a school teacher, in Whitehaven.

BRITISH CROWN GREEN ASSOCIATION. THE COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP. LANCASHIRE REGAIN THE HONOURS. The last of the 1912 series of the inter-county con- tests for the championship of the British Crown Green Amateur Association was held on Saturday, Lancashire defeating Staffordshire at the Angel Hotel, Stretford, and Heath Town, Staffordshire and Derbyshire, losing to Cheshire at Davenham and Chinley.
